&lt;p&gt;The checkpoint includes the MTP head, but the JumpStart serving configuration does not enable speculative decoding.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploy-nemotron-3.5-super-vl-with-sagemaker-jumpstart"&gt;Deploy Nemotron 3.5 Super VL with SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can deploy the model from SageMaker Studio, with the SageMaker Python SDK, or from the Hugging Face model page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you deploy, make sure you have the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions to create Amazon SageMaker AI endpoints.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role with access to SageMaker AI.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Service quota for ml.g7.24xlarge, ml.g7.48xlarge, or ml.p5en.48xlarge endpoint usage. The ml.g7 quotas start at zero in new accounts, so request an increase through Service Quotas before you deploy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For the SDK path, the SageMaker Python SDK v3 (version 3.25 or later).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-from-sagemaker-studio"&gt;Deploy from SageMaker Studio&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy the model from SageMaker Studio, complete the following steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open SageMaker Studio and, in the navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;JumpStart&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the search bar, enter Nemotron 3.5 Super VL. The search results list both variants, as shown in the following screenshot. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-1.png" alt="SageMaker JumpStart search results listing the two NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL variants" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Searching for NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the model card for the variant you want to deploy. Choose NVFP4 for optimized inference on G7 instances. The following screenshot shows the NVFP4 model card. 
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-2.png" alt="NVFP4 model card for NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL in SageMaker JumpStart" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL NVFP4 model card in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Choose BF16 if you need the full-precision reference weights on ml.p5en.48xlarge. The following screenshot shows the BF16 model card.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-3.png" alt="BF16 model card for NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL in SageMaker JumpStart" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL BF16 model card in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the model card, review the model details and license terms, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;. The following screenshot shows the model details you can review before deployment. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-4.png" alt="Model details and capabilities page for Nemotron 3.5 Super VL before deployment" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Reviewing Nemotron 3.5 Super VL capabilities and model details before deployment&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Instance type&lt;/strong&gt;, keep the default (ml.g7.24xlarge for NVFP4 or ml.p5en.48xlarge for BF16), or choose another supported instance type. Then choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Wait for the endpoint status to change to InService before you invoke the model. Deployment takes about 10 minutes on ml.g7.24xlarge. The following screenshot shows a deployment in progress. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-5.png" alt="SageMaker endpoint deployment in progress before the status reaches InService" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: SageMaker endpoint deployment in progress before the status reaches InService&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-with-the-sagemaker-python-sdk"&gt;Deploy with the SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Install the SageMaker Python SDK v3: pip install "sagemaker&amp;gt;=3.25"
from sagemaker.core.helper.session_helper import get_execution_role
from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.configs import JumpStartConfig
from sagemaker.core.training.configs import Compute
from sagemaker.serve import ModelBuilder

# BF16 variant: model_id "huggingface-vlm-nvidia-nemotron-3-5-super-vl-120b-a12b-bf16" on ml.p5en.48xlarge.
jumpstart_config = JumpStartConfig(
    model_id="huggingface-vlm-nvidia-nemotron-3-5-super-vl-120b-a12b-nvfp4",
    inference_config_name="modify_best_price_performance",
)
model_builder = ModelBuilder.from_jumpstart_config(
    jumpstart_config=jumpstart_config,
    role_arn=get_execution_role(),
    compute=Compute(instance_type="ml.g7.24xlarge", instance_count=1),
)
model_builder.build()
endpoint = model_builder.deploy(endpoint_name="nemotron-3-5-super-vl")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The example uses the SageMaker Python SDK v3 classes: &lt;code&gt;JumpStartConfig&lt;/code&gt; names the model, &lt;code&gt;ModelBuilder&lt;/code&gt; builds and deploys it, and &lt;code&gt;deploy&lt;/code&gt; returns an &lt;code&gt;Endpoint&lt;/code&gt; resource. Pass the instance type in &lt;code&gt;Compute&lt;/code&gt;, because the SDK falls back to ml.m5.large when the instance type is absent, and the build then fails. The deploy call waits until the endpoint is InService, which takes about 10 minutes on ml.g7.24xlarge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;inference_config_name&lt;/code&gt; argument selects a serving configuration, and each configuration sets a different context length. The default generate configurations serve a 2,275-token context sized for short prompts. The summarize configurations serve 13,650 tokens, and the modify configurations serve 15,600 tokens. The example uses a modify configuration because the inference examples in this post, especially image, video, and reasoning requests, need the larger context. All the inference examples that follow assume the modify configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-from-hugging-face"&gt;Deploy from Hugging Face&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also deploy the model from its Hugging Face model page: choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;, and continue to the SageMaker deployment workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-6.png" alt="Hugging Face model page for NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL with the Deploy menu open" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL model page on Hugging Face with the Deploy menu&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the deployment menu, choose &lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt; to open the SageMaker deployment workflow, as shown in the following screenshot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22096-7.png" alt="Amazon SageMaker AI option selected in the Hugging Face deployment workflow" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Selecting Amazon SageMaker AI from the Hugging Face deployment workflow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the endpoint is InService, you can send inference requests to it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-inference"&gt;Run inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The JumpStart endpoint exposes a vLLM-powered chat completions API, so applications can send inference requests using a familiar chat-message format. NVIDIA recommends temperature 1.0 and &lt;code&gt;top_p&lt;/code&gt; 0.95 for text, and temperature 0.6 and &lt;code&gt;top_p&lt;/code&gt; 0.95 for image and video requests. You must send media inline as base64 data URLs because the endpoint runs with network isolation and cannot fetch URLs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="text-with-reasoning-on"&gt;Text, with reasoning on&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reasoning is on by default, and you can configure it per request. The reasoning text is returned in the reasoning field of the message, separate from content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json

payload = {
    "messages": [{"role": "user", "content":
        "Explain the difference between OCR and document understanding."}],
    "max_tokens": 2000,
    "temperature": 1.0,
    "top_p": 0.95,
}
response = endpoint.invoke(
    body=json.dumps(payload), content_type="application/json", accept="application/json"
)
result = json.loads(response.body.read())
print(result["choices"][0]["message"]["reasoning"])
print(result["choices"][0]["message"]["content"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="image-with-reasoning-off"&gt;Image, with reasoning off&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For direct extraction-style tasks, you can disable reasoning with &lt;code&gt;enable_thinking=False&lt;/code&gt; in &lt;code&gt;chat_template_kwargs&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import base64

with open("invoice.png", "rb") as f:
    image_b64 = base64.b64encode(f.read()).decode("utf-8")

payload = {
    "messages": [{
        "role": "user",
        "content": [
            {"type": "image_url",
             "image_url": {"url": f"data:image/png;base64,{image_b64}"}},
            {"type": "text",
             "text": "Extract the vendor, date, and total as JSON."},
        ],
    }],
    "max_tokens": 1024,
    "temperature": 0.6,
    "top_p": 0.95,
    "chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": False},
}
response = endpoint.invoke(
    body=json.dumps(payload), content_type="application/json", accept="application/json"
)
result = json.loads(response.body.read())
print(result["choices"][0]["message"]["content"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model accepts images rather than raw PDF files. To process a PDF, render each page to an image such as PNG before sending it to the model. A 1,536 x 1,024 page costs about 1,536 tokens of context, and one request can carry one image.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="video"&gt;Video&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example sends a short MP4 clip as a base64-encoded data URL and asks the model to summarize it, with reasoning disabled for a direct response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import base64

with open("demo.mp4", "rb") as f:
    video_b64 = base64.b64encode(f.read()).decode("utf-8")

payload = {
    "messages": [{
        "role": "user",
        "content": [
            {"type": "video_url",
             "video_url": {"url": f"data:video/mp4;base64,{video_b64}"}},
            {"type": "text", "text": "Summarize what happens in this video."},
        ],
    }],
    "max_tokens": 2000,
    "temperature": 0.6,
    "top_p": 0.95,
    "chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": False},
}
response = endpoint.invoke(
    body=json.dumps(payload), content_type="application/json", accept="application/json"
)
result = json.loads(response.body.read())
print(result["choices"][0]["message"]["content"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For video inference, use short, compressed clips and keep the encoded request within the endpoint’s supported payload limits. For reasoning-intensive video requests, set &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; high enough to allow the model to complete its reasoning while considering response length and endpoint invocation limits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices"&gt;Best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider the following recommendations when you run Nemotron 3.5 Super VL on SageMaker JumpStart:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Media inputs&lt;/strong&gt;: The model accepts images rather than raw PDF files, so render PDF pages to images first.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Video configuration&lt;/strong&gt;: Frame sampling is set when the endpoint launches, not per request. The JumpStart endpoint uses the vLLM default sampling and rejects per-request &lt;code&gt;media_io_kwargs&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Video with reasoning&lt;/strong&gt;: Set &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; to at least 8,192 for video with reasoning enabled, or disable reasoning when you want a direct response.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Context budget&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; caps the completion. Prompt and completion together must fit in the serving configuration’s model length: 2,275 tokens for the generate configurations, 520 for interact, 13,650 for summarize, and 15,600 for modify. Images and video frames also consume context.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multipage documents&lt;/strong&gt;: The JumpStart serving configuration accepts one image per request. Send one page per request, or stitch pages into one image, and check the context budget.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reasoning mode&lt;/strong&gt;: Use reasoning for multi-step analysis and agentic tasks. Disable it for extraction or captioning when you want a shorter, direct response.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sampling&lt;/strong&gt;: Start with temperature 1.0 and &lt;code&gt;top_p&lt;/code&gt; 0.95 for text, and temperature 0.6 and &lt;code&gt;top_p&lt;/code&gt; 0.95 for image and video, as NVIDIA recommends.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the endpoint, the endpoint configuration, and the model when you are done:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.core.resources import EndpointConfig, Model

endpoint_config = EndpointConfig.get(endpoint_config_name=endpoint.endpoint_config_name)
model_name = endpoint_config.production_variants[0].model_name
endpoint.delete()
endpoint_config.delete()
Model.get(model_name=model_name).delete()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Super VL brings image, video, and document understanding to SageMaker JumpStart. The NVFP4 variant is available on G7 instances with NVIDIA RTX PRO 4500 Blackwell Server Edition GPUs. The BF16 variant is available on ml.p5en.48xlarge with NVIDIA H200 GPUs. Choose NVFP4 for optimized inference with model quality close to the BF16 checkpoint, or BF16 when you want the full-precision reference weights. To get started, open SageMaker JumpStart in SageMaker Studio or use the SageMaker Python SDK.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see SageMaker JumpStart pretrained models in the Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide and the &lt;a href="https://research.nvidia.com/labs/nemotron/files/NVIDIA-Nemotron-3.5-Super-and-Lightning-Technical-Report.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Nemotron 3.5 Super VL technical report&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Pluggable Agentic AI Framework: A seven-layer composable enterprise agentic AI reference architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/pluggable-agentic-ai-framework-a-seven-layer-composable-enterprise-agentic-ai-reference-architecture-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sindhura Palakodety]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 09 Nov 2026 19:05:54 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Thought Leadership]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">333ca621d84290b49ff9a6ae139d900744bc2faa</guid>

					<description>A seven-layer composable reference architecture for taking enterprise agentic AI from prototype to production on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Each layer has a stable contract, so you can swap technologies at any layer without redesigning the rest of the stack.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;“How do we run our AI agents in production at scale?” is the question every customer eventually asks. It comes bundled with several questions. How do we handle thousands of concurrent user requests securely and confidently? How do we make sure the agents stay reliable under real traffic instead of failing silently? How do we know if an agent still meets the quality and safety standards it passed during testing? What are agent security best practices? Should that be deterministic or non-deterministic? And many more. A proof-of-concept answers none of these, because it is built to show the agent model can do the task, not to survive production traffic, cost pressure, and security review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That gap between a working prototype and a system that runs reliably, securely, frugally and responsibly at scale, is where most agentic AI initiatives stall, and it is rarely the model’s fault. Every team building an agent tends to rebuild the same scaffolding from scratch: its own routing logic, its own guardrails, its own observability, all applied after the fact rather than built in from the start. The teams need a shared, production-ready architecture that every agent can build on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post walks through a seven-layer composable reference architecture designed to take multi-agent AI applications from prototype to production scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="composable-architecture"&gt;Composable architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Pluggable Agentic AI Framework is a reference architecture for multi-agent applications organized into &lt;strong&gt;seven layers&lt;/strong&gt;, each with a distinct responsibility and a stable contract. The services and technologies shown in each layer reflect the reference implementation, not mandatory choices. Organizations can substitute alternatives that align with their existing infrastructure and requirements. Together, these layers offer a blueprint for building, securing, and operating agentic AI applications at production scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22256-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22256-1.png" alt="The seven layers of the Pluggable Agentic AI Framework, from data and knowledge up through agentic operations" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The seven layers of the Pluggable Agentic AI Framework&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data and Knowledge&lt;/strong&gt; is the foundation of Agentic AI. An agent is only as good as the &lt;strong&gt;data&lt;/strong&gt; it can access. Whether an agent is checking out a healthcare formulary, tracking inventory, or interpreting a compliance rule, it needs relevant, up-to-date information to answer correctly. This layer owns retrieval, embeddings, and access to structured and unstructured data through a clear contract and protocol: accept a query and return the most relevant information that acts as context for an agent. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/serverless-vector-database/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Vector Engine for Amazon OpenSearch Serverless&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/features/vectors/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt; are example building blocks for this foundation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model and Inference&lt;/strong&gt; choices should be driven by configuration, not hardwired into agent or application logic. Use smaller, faster models for simple tasks and larger models for requests that require more nuanced reasoning. It is important to conduct a thorough model evaluation for the use case you are building with realistic and representative business data. A model routing layer keeps AI agents decoupled from the model hosting environment and provider-specific APIs by relying on a consistent request-and-response contract. A proxy such as &lt;a href="https://www.litellm.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LiteLLM&lt;/a&gt; provides a common interface across providers, so you can switch models based on your evaluations without rewriting application logic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Orchestration&lt;/strong&gt; determines how work is distributed, and choosing the right approach is critical. For straightforward tasks, use a single agent to handle the work from end to end. For complex, cross-domain workflows, use a supervisor to delegate specialist agents, each scoped to a specific capability and tied to a specific set of tools. Avoid building one agent that does everything. As tools, instructions, and context accumulate, the context window grows, latency increases, and accuracy can suffer. Tool sprawl sets in, which can push agents beyond their intended boundaries, leading to unintended actions and security breaches. Splitting responsibilities keeps each agent’s context focused and makes latency more predictable. You can also test each agent’s behavior, scale it, and define its security model independently. Enforce bounded autonomy: specialists should execute strictly within the tools they have been granted, and nothing more.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition to decomposing into specific specialists and setting its boundary, decouple the specialist agents from the supervisor agents. Allow the supervisor to discover the specialists from a centralized agent registry and invoke them through a standardized protocol like MCP (Model Context Protocol) and A2A (Agent to Agent). Maintain a centralized agent registry that catalogs every agent’s tools, skills, and owner. This gives your organization the visibility it needs for governance at scale, increases the velocity of AI agent implementation through reusability, and avoids the cost of repetitive development. Consider using &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt; for specialists, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/harness.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore harness&lt;/a&gt; for the orchestrator, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt; for centralized discovery and routing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security and Guardrails&lt;/strong&gt; should be a primary concern, built into the design from day one. Agentic AI exposes organizations to a new class of risks that conventional security frameworks were not built to address, from prompt injection and tool misuse to memory and data poisoning. Treat all external content as untrusted by default. Authenticate every request, whether it comes from a user, another agent, or an automated workflow, through a service like &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;. Then use fine-grained access control to determine whether each tool call is permitted before execution. Grant agents temporary, scoped credentials instead of persistent secrets. On the data-protection side, mask sensitive data like PHI (Personal Health Information) and PII (Personally Identifiable Information) in agent and tool requests and responses, so that sensitive customer information does not appear in agent outputs or lingers in memory. For accountability, capture every significant decision in a tamper-evident audit trail, route high-impact actions through a human approval gate, and deploy circuit breakers to halt anomalous behavior before it can escalate. Consider using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; for user inputs, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; for authentication and access control, along with AWS Security and governance controls to bring defense-in-depth security.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application&lt;/strong&gt; is where the end-user experience lives, whether that is a chat interface, an API surface, or an embedded widget. Keep this layer intentionally thin. Its only responsibilities should be presenting the conversation and passing requests to the orchestration layer beneath it. Resist the urge to embed business logic here. The cleaner the separation, the more easily you can iterate on the user experience without destabilizing the backend. A decision model can sit at this boundary as a fast, inexpensive check: in one call it scores whether a request is in scope, needs a human, or is urgent, returning probabilities instead of free text. Your code acts on those scores with simple thresholds, answering off-topic requests directly and flagging escalations, so the full agentic loop runs only when it adds value. When building the application layer, think about what would be needed for another AI agent to consume and interact with your AI agents along with humans. Build your external interfaces accordingly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observability&lt;/strong&gt; should watch every layer from day one, not after the first incident. Along with the logs and metrics, end-to-end agent traces are critical to measure whether the agent performed its task correctly. Along with the trace, collect cost and token usage per request, time to first token, whether the call is secure, and surface dashboards for latency, error rates, and spend before you go live. Proactive monitoring is cheaper than reactive firefighting. Consider using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; with OpenTelemetry along with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/omni/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Omni&lt;/a&gt; for tracing and metrics to get comprehensive visibility across your stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agentic Operations&lt;/strong&gt; closes the loop with continuous evaluation, so set it up from day one. Score every production session for groundedness, accuracy, and safety, and put a regression gate in place to catch quality drops before they reach customers. Without this, you only know the agent worked at launch. With it, you can prove it still works after every subsequent release. Consider using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt; for scoring and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/optimization.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore optimization&lt;/a&gt; for continuous performance tuning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="reference-implementation-for-a-retail-customer-support-agent"&gt;Reference implementation for a retail customer support agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22256-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22256-2.png" alt="Functional flow of the retail customer support agent, from shopper request through supervisor routing to the order and refund specialist agents" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Functional flow of the retail customer support reference implementation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To bring the architecture to life, the framework includes a ready-to-run reference implementation based on a retail customer support scenario covering order status and refund eligibility. We chose this use case because it exercises every layer of the stack while remaining intuitive enough for a team to follow and adapt to their own needs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A supervisor agent receives a customer’s refund request and delegates to an Order agent and a Refund agent, each a specialist with a scoped set of tools. The Order agent looks up delivery and purchase details from Amazon DynamoDB. The Refund agent retrieves tier-based policy rules from a knowledge base and calculates eligibility using the delivery date, the current date, and the customer’s loyalty tier. Every one of these calls passes through the AgentCore Gateway, which enforces fine-grained access control before a tool executes with Policy in AgentCore, and every call is traced end-to-end through the observability layer, so when a calculation goes wrong, the trace shows exactly which layer and which decision point caused it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Anyone building a similar supervisor-and-specialist pattern can clone the reference implementation and swap in their own domain logic layer by layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-pluggability-matters-as-much-as-the-layers-themselves"&gt;Why pluggability matters as much as the layers themselves&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The value of a seven-layer architecture lies not only in how it separates responsibilities, but in how quickly each layer can evolve. &lt;strong&gt;Pluggability&lt;/strong&gt; makes that possible. Stable contracts at layer boundaries, from identity checks and access policies to telemetry interfaces, let you change the underlying technology without redesigning the rest of the stack. Essentially, take an architecture-first approach to building agentic AI systems rather than a technology-driven approach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The framework puts this into practice by demonstrating pluggability through integrations with third-party providers across the layers: model and hosting providers at the inference layer, search capabilities at the orchestration layer, decision APIs at the application layer, guardrails at the security layer, and agent tracing and monitoring solutions at the observability layer alongside business value and cost tracking. It supports industry-standard providers at each layer, so you can compose solutions that meet your specific requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For enterprises, flexibility protects existing investments. Teams can retain the observability, security, and inference tools they already trust, fill gaps where needed, and adopt new capabilities incrementally. The architecture provides a consistent foundation without requiring every technology decision to come from the same vendor.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A working prototype proves what an agent can do. A well-designed architecture determines whether your enterprise can depend on it. Moving to AI operations means designing security, governance, visibility, cost controls, reliability, and adaptability into the design from day one, not adding them after the system is built.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Pluggable Agentic AI Framework brings those concerns into an architecture with a clear principle: build a stable foundation without locking every technology choice in place. Models will change. Tools will evolve. Business requirements will shift. Your architecture should make those changes manageable, not turn each one into a rebuild.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Put that principle into practice. Start with the &lt;a href="https://catalog.us-east-1.prod.workshops.aws/workshops/a782d075-7943-4251-9d8f-58c428b119a9" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Workshop Studio experience&lt;/a&gt;, explore the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-pluggable-agentic-ai-framework" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;public sample repository&lt;/a&gt;, and use the hands-on implementation as a starting point for your own architecture. It is not a substitute for production security hardening, testing, and review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Build the capabilities you need today and the &lt;strong&gt;flexibility&lt;/strong&gt; you will need tomorrow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How CrossnoKaye grounds physical AI with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-crossnokaye-grounds-physical-ai-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Raphael Simon]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 09 Nov 2026 18:55:43 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">08caff2b840ccbd409e94a190d5802d3f577d510</guid>

					<description>CrossnoKaye brought physical AI to heavy industry with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Its ATLAS platform and AURA agentic system ground AI reasoning in live plant data and run verifiable Python in isolated AgentCore sandboxes, giving plant engineers checkable, math-backed answers instead of model guesses.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Industrial operations present AI challenges that don’t show up in typical enterprise software. In most facilities, plant data and control logic are spread across separate systems from different vendors: programmable logic controllers (PLCs) and distributed control systems (DCS) run the equipment logic, historians record time-series tags under site-specific naming schemes, supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) systems drive operator screens, and standalone optimization tools or alarm loggers sit on the side. Bolting an external AI agent onto that stack rarely works well in practice. Each system uses its own naming conventions. The logic that actually governs how equipment behaves (the control sequences, state machines, and safety interlocks) is locked inside proprietary controller code that an external tool can’t inspect. Giving a bolt-on agent access across multiple operational technology (OT) systems also means distributing credentials across the plant network.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://crossnokaye.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CrossnoKaye&lt;/a&gt; took a different approach to bring physical AI to heavy industry using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;. Its software-defined automation platform, ATLAS, connects live plant data and AI reasoning to closed-loop control of the equipment running food and beverage plants, industrial refrigeration facilities, aggregates quarries, and manufacturing sites. Across more than 100 industrial facilities and 13,000 controlled assets, CrossnoKaye stores over 22 trillion machine data points, ingests more than 50 billion new readings every day, and runs over 1,000 live advanced process control (APC) applications; CrossnoKaye reports that these applications improve production throughput by 11–17 percent while reducing total facility energy costs by an average of 21 percent. Because ATLAS already manages the facility model, the base control software, the historian, and the optimization applications in one platform on AWS, CrossnoKaye’s agentic intelligence system, AURA, has native access to the full operational state of the plant within a single security boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Having the plant context is only half the problem, though. Large language models (LLMs) are good at interpreting engineering context and planning an investigation, but they cannot reliably compute statistics or physical balances over millions of sensor readings in a prompt. To back every numerical answer with real math, AURA uses the code interpreter capability in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to write and run Python workloads at runtime inside isolated, AWS managed microVM sandboxes, alongside &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; and local edge safety controls. This post covers how CrossnoKaye architected ATLAS and AURA on AWS, how AURA uses Amazon Bedrock AgentCore across interactive Chats, repeatable Tasks, and automated Routines, and what the team learned running physical AI in production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ATLAS controls industrial processes across existing PLC and DCS infrastructure, from kilns, crushers, drying rooms, ovens, and spiral freezers to chillers, turbines, valves, and compressors, without requiring customers to rip and replace plant hardware. Figure 1 illustrates how on-premises ATLAS edge clusters, continuous cloud optimization, and AURA work together on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-22370-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-22370-1.png" alt="CrossnoKaye ATLAS edge clusters stream plant telemetry to AWS, where cloud optimization and AURA agents run analysis in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: CrossnoKaye AURA and ATLAS physical AI architecture with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ATLAS and AURA are built around three core layers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, the plant is defined in software from the edge up. At each facility, an on-premises ATLAS control panel with redundant computers, power supplies, and network switches (so local control continues uninterrupted while a failed hardware component is replaced) runs a local Kubernetes cluster that uses contract-first microservices, extending the ATLAS cloud platform on AWS to the plant floor. In the cloud, ATLAS authors, validates, and versions the facility’s base control narratives (defining equipment state machines, sequences, operating bounds, interlocks, and alarm rules), which are downloaded to the edge appliances and run locally against existing equipment and controllers. CrossnoKaye reports over 9,000 control software deployments per year and more than 1 million run hours with zero safety incidents. Each edge appliance also acts as an outbound-only information technology/operational technology (IT/OT) gateway that opens zero inbound firewall ports inside the plant, streaming high-frequency telemetry to AWS through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/lattice/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon VPC Lattice&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/msk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka (Amazon MSK)&lt;/a&gt;. If cloud connectivity drops, the plant continues running safely on the base control narratives on the local edge cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Second, derived engineering signals and closed-loop optimization applications run on top of base control using the same shared plant model. In the cloud, CrossnoKaye’s Scheduling system runs two continuous pipelines from versioned Python packages in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;, operating on configurable per-instance cadences and event-driven triggers when plant settings change. Synthetic reading algorithms compute derived engineering metrics, such as heat-flow distributions, asset health indices, energy intensity, and mass-balance deviations, and write them back to the historian alongside physical I/O. Closed-loop optimization applications run on top of the base control layer, combining those derived synthetic readings with physics models, model predictive control (MPC), reinforcement learning, utility tariffs, and weather forecasts to compute optimal setpoint schedules. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sqs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS)&lt;/a&gt; FIFO queues order every schedule and state update by facility before dispatching setpoints to the local base control narrative on the edge cluster, which CrossnoKaye reports runs 650,000 closed-loop control actions per day.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Third, AURA runs inside the platform boundary on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)&lt;/a&gt; as microservices and durable &lt;a href="https://temporal.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Temporal&lt;/a&gt; workflows. Under the caller’s enterprise permissions, AURA agents query the internal Atlas Data gateway to read the operational state of a facility or fleet in one consistent model: upstream and downstream equipment topology, the deployed base control narratives and interlocks, physical I/O and synthetic readings, setting change and alarm histories, active optimization schedules, and plant documentation indexed in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Having the equipment layout, the base control logic, and the sensor data in the same system makes a concrete difference when diagnosing plant issues. At an aggregates quarry running primary, secondary, and tertiary rock crushers, the back half of the plant tripped twice in a single shift. From the control room, operators saw the secondary crusher fault, cleared the alarm, and restarted the line, only for it to trip again hours later. In the historian timestamps alone, the secondary crusher faulted seconds after the tertiary crusher, which made it look like a crusher problem. Because AURA also reads the conveyor layout and the deployed base control interlocks, its investigation followed the actual control logic. A recirculating belt beneath the tertiary crusher was slipping, which tripped the tertiary crusher’s overload interlock. That interlock’s upstream safety interlock immediately halted the secondary crusher to prevent burying a stopped downstream crusher in stone. Instead of chasing a phantom crusher fault, the plant manager sent an operator to inspect the recirculating belt, confirming and fixing the slip in minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The numbered flows in Figure 1 trace how an AURA investigation runs on AWS:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Client and edge ingress: Engineers and Model Context Protocol (MCP) clients connect through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/waf/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF&lt;/a&gt; and a public Application Load Balancer, where the API gateway validates enterprise single sign-on (SSO) or MCP credentials. Edge telemetry and control schedules flow through Amazon VPC Lattice (authenticated with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) Signature Version 4 (SigV4)) and Amazon MSK.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Context retrieval and guarded inference: AURA workflows read plant state through the Atlas Data gateway and route prompts and organization-scoped Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) queries through the Model Gateway to foundation models, Amazon Bedrock Guardrails, and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Runtime computation dispatch: For statistical, thermodynamic, or multi-dataset math, the workflow streams input datasets over gRPC to AURA’s internal Sandbox Orchestrator service.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Session coordination and live streaming: In interactive sessions, the Sandbox Orchestrator tracks active sandbox session IDs and streams live progress through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticache/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ElastiCache&lt;/a&gt; for Valkey.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Isolated Python execution: The Sandbox Orchestrator uses the AWS SDK to call the endpoint for the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, staging datasets and running model-authored Python inside an isolated code interpreter microVM.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Artifact storage and Brief delivery: Verified Apache Parquet outputs, charts, and cited Briefs are saved to Amazon S3 over &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/privatelink/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS PrivateLink&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ses/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Email Service (Amazon SES)&lt;/a&gt; delivers automated Briefs to plant teams.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AURA combines foundation models on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; (for model availability by AWS Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide) with isolated Python sandboxes in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Code Interpreter, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, across three operational modes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Chats: Interactive, multi-turn engineering conversations spanning a single unit, a process line, or a multi-site portfolio (up to 200 facilities in a single turn).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tasks: Repeatable, parameterized investigations bound to immutable version snapshots that produce structured technical Briefs with cited evidence tables and charts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Routines: Automated background watchers triggered by schedules, sensor thresholds, rate-of-change windows, alarms, or equipment mode transitions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections examine four architectural decisions behind these workflows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="warm-conversational-sandboxes-compared-to-clean-single-run-pipelines"&gt;Warm conversational sandboxes compared to clean single-run pipelines&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Interactive debugging and automated plant reporting have different state requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In an interactive Chat, an engineer explores hypotheses step by step: loading 24 hours of critical alarms across 94 facilities, filtering out chattering tank-level sensors at one site, and correlating compressor trips against ambient wet-bulb temperatures. Starting a cold sandbox on every turn would wipe in-memory tables and add latency. For Chats, the Sandbox Orchestrator binds one warm Amazon Bedrock AgentCore session to the conversation in Amazon ElastiCache for Valkey with a 15-minute sliding time to live (TTL), transparently provisioning a replacement session if an engineer returns after the idle timeout expires.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In a scheduled Task or a 2:00 AM Routine, reproducibility comes first. Every run must start in a clean environment with no leftover state from prior runs. For Tasks and Routines, the Sandbox Orchestrator provisions a fresh, single-use AgentCore microVM per run and stops the session with &lt;code&gt;StopCodeInterpreterSession&lt;/code&gt; as soon as the calculation finishes, preventing both cross-run contamination and idle sandbox charges.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This warm-session design makes fleet-wide triage practical in a single conversation. When an operations director asks AURA to review all critical alarms across 94 facilities over the past 24 hours and identify the primary mechanical risks, the agent loads the fleet alarm summaries from the Atlas Data gateway into its AgentCore sandbox and groups activations by facility, rule, and inter-arrival time. Rather than ranking sites by raw alarm count, the Python analysis separates a plant where 103 critical activations came from two repeating dump-tank level rules from a plant where 58 activations spanned ammonia detection, evaporator faults, and hot-gas valves within one second of each other, pointing safety and maintenance teams directly to the facility with cascading equipment faults.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="verifiable-computation-instead-of-unverified-model-math"&gt;Verifiable computation instead of unverified model math&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When an engineer asks AURA to analyze a process trend or audit shift performance, the numbers in the response must be exact. Early in AURA’s development, the team found that letting a model write free-form Python scripts that print JSON or text summaries to standard output was too brittle: a model would occasionally misname a column, drop part of a time window, or format output in a way that broke downstream chart rendering.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To make runtime computation verifiable, AURA enforces strict data contracts around every Amazon Bedrock AgentCore run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Upfront contract validation and multi-source staging: Before allocating a sandbox session, the Sandbox Orchestrator validates the input contract and stages up to 50 datasets into the microVM workspace, combining physical I/O, derived synthetic readings, setting logs, alarm histories, and utility rate tables.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In-sandbox DataFrame schema verification: Rather than parsing free-form text from standard output, the service appends a deterministic verification harness to the model’s Python script. Inside the sandbox, the harness verifies that every declared output table exists in memory as a &lt;code&gt;pandas.DataFrame&lt;/code&gt; with all required columns and types, and serializes the verified tables directly to binary Apache Parquet files.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automated self-correction: If the script raises a Python exception or omits a required column, no unverified numbers reach the user. The Temporal workflow captures the traceback and schema mismatch as a structured retry prompt so the agent fixes its Python logic and re-runs the calculation in a clean sandbox before publishing any conclusion.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="automated-night-shift-routines-and-cited-briefs-through-amazon-ses"&gt;Automated night-shift Routines and cited Briefs through Amazon SES&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Plant engineers cannot watch dashboards across 100 facilities around the clock, and many equipment issues start as slow drift on the night shift. AURA Routines run in the background, watching for specific process conditions (such as suction-pressure drift on a refrigeration stage, a batch drying curve falling behind specification, or an unscheduled manual setpoint override) and launching an investigation automatically when one triggers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A night-shift catch on an industrial chiller plant shows how this works end to end:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automated detection and sandboxed root-cause analysis (02:00–02:03 AM): At 2:00 AM, a Routine detects low-stage suction-pressure drift and launches its pinned Task in read-only mode. Inside an Amazon Bedrock AgentCore sandbox, the agent correlates condenser approach temperatures against low-stage suction pressure across physical and synthetic readings, rules out sensor drift and low refrigerant charge, and isolates condenser-side fouling.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deterministic citation validation (02:04 AM): Before publishing the Brief, AURA parses every inline citation and verifies that each claim links to a saved telemetry window, setting change record, or sandbox output artifact in Amazon S3. No control change is made automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Brief delivery through Amazon SES and one-click Chat handoff: Within four minutes of the trigger, AURA sends a formatted HTML Brief email through Amazon SES to the plant engineering team with the diagnosis, cited signal traces, and an “Investigate in AURA” button. When the lead engineer selects that button before the morning shift huddle, AURA verifies the engineer’s live facility permissions and opens an interactive Chat pre-loaded with the Brief’s evidence so the team can ask follow-up questions or issue a work order immediately.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="defense-in-depth-industrial-safety"&gt;Defense in depth industrial safety&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In an industrial plant, operational safety cannot depend on an LLM following instructions in a prompt. Because ATLAS and AURA share the same platform from the cloud down to the edge Kubernetes cluster on the plant floor, CrossnoKaye enforces strict boundaries on what AI and optimization workloads can do across four independent layers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails and verifiable provenance (Observe): Every prompt and completion routed through AURA’s Model Gateway is evaluated by Amazon Bedrock Guardrails to enforce content, topic, and safety policies. Knowledge Base retrieval is isolated by organization, and every Brief requires verified inline citations, noting both which time windows were analyzed and any periods where sensor data was unavailable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Air-gapped read-only sandboxes and human-approved proposals (Propose): Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Code Interpreter microVMs run in isolated, AWS managed environments with no network routes, credentials, or API paths to plant equipment. Automated Routines run strictly in read-only mode. When an engineer uses an interactive session to propose a setting change, the platform requires explicit human approval under the operator’s corporate identity and verifies the physical outcome in plant telemetry afterward.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Non-bypassable deterministic Level 1 base control at the edge (Bounded and Base Control): Cloud optimization applications don’t drive field hardware directly. They run on top of the platform’s base control layer by dispatching setpoint schedules within engineering-approved envelopes. On the plant floor, the edge Kubernetes cluster runs the downloaded base control narratives locally in real time, enforcing deterministic state-machine guards, rate-of-change clamps, operating bounds, and equipment interlocks that cannot be bypassed by cloud services, optimization applications, or AI agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Independent physical hardware protection: Beneath the ATLAS software-defined controller, the plant’s physical machinery and field wiring enforce independent, hardwired protection layers, including safety relays, local programmable trips, and mechanical pressure-relief and over-temperature devices.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-and-lessons-learned"&gt;Results and lessons learned&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across food processing, industrial refrigeration, aggregates and mining, and manufacturing sites, CrossnoKaye reports that combining software-defined edge control, continuous cloud optimization, and AURA has reduced batch drying-time standard deviation by 34 percent (with 6 percent more product finishing within target yield specification) and identified more than 50,000 tons per year of recoverable production gap in quarry operations. With over 5,000 AI agents monitoring more than 10,000 plant assets across customer facilities (including over 13,000 controlled devices and equipment units across the ATLAS fleet), investigations that previously required hours of exporting historian CSVs, cross-referencing PLC ladder logic, and building spreadsheets now complete in under four minutes, delivering cited technical Briefs through Amazon SES before the morning shift meeting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three lessons stood out from building and operating AURA across industrial facilities:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Unify the plant model and control logic before adding AI: An agent can only diagnose real physical root causes, like distinguishing a slipping downstream recirculating belt from a secondary crusher fault, when it can read the process topology, the deployed base control narratives and interlocks, the setting change history, and the optimizer state in one consistent model rather than stitching together six disconnected plant systems.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deliver value inside the operator’s daily workflow, not as a separate tool to go use: Plant teams are busy running equipment. If an AI assistant is a separate destination where engineers must start from a blank prompt, day-to-day adoption stalls. Embedding AURA into the daily shift rhythm (where background Routines catch anomalies overnight, run the Python analysis in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and deliver a cited Brief to the engineer’s inbox with a one-click handoff into a pre-loaded Chat) means operators see the value in their normal routine without having to go use a separate product.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enforce data, coverage, and safety contracts in code rather than prompts: In a plant, a missing hour of sensor data isn’t the same as an hour with no events, and an unverified calculation can send a maintenance crew to the wrong machine. Making data-coverage gaps explicit in tool contracts, verifying &lt;code&gt;pandas.DataFrame&lt;/code&gt; schemas inside the AgentCore sandbox before extracting binary Apache Parquet outputs, and enforcing deterministic base control bounds on the edge Kubernetes cluster catch probabilistic model errors before they reach an operator or a piece of equipment.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reliable physical AI in heavy industry requires grounding models in the actual equipment topology and control logic of the plant, and backing every quantitative claim with verifiable Python calculations. Together with Amazon Bedrock Guardrails and isolated Python sandboxes in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, ATLAS and AURA allow agents to reason over live facility ontology, base control narratives, setting histories, physical I/O, optimization state, and synthetic readings, delivering checkable engineering answers and cited technical Briefs through Amazon SES while keeping deterministic safety controls firmly on the plant floor.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about building agents with isolated code execution, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt; or visit &lt;a href="https://crossnokaye.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CrossnoKaye&lt;/a&gt; to see how physical AI is transforming heavy industry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

Across virtually every industry, leading enterprises including 3M, BMW, Mondelez, New York Life, and Southwest Airlines are using Quick to transform how they work. &lt;p&gt;&lt;iframe loading="lazy" title="AWS Quick: Built for the Job | Amazon Web Services" width="500" height="281" src="https://www.youtube-nocookie.com/embed/X93DOtA7dpA?feature=oembed" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen sandbox="allow-scripts allow-same-origin"&gt;&lt;/iframe&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-work-you-know"&gt;The work you know&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customers tell us their AI assistants make individuals faster, but the gains stay personal. A sales rep still builds territory plans by pulling from the CRM, dashboards, call notes, and email. Because context stays locked inside a single project or one vendor’s suite, productivity never compounds across the team or the company. Most of those tools started as consumer apps and still serve personal and professional use with the same product. AWS Quick took the opposite path: it was built for work from day one, on the same AWS infrastructure that millions of customers use to run their mission-critical applications. These include many of the world’s most complex and security-sensitive workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="built-for-your-business-from-day-one"&gt;Built for your business from day one&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Quick has no consumer version. Every roadmap decision and every feature we ship starts with one question: does it help individuals, teams, and entire enterprises do their most important work? Quick knows your role, your team, and how your projects, people, and data connect, because it works across the applications and workflows your teams already use. Quick watches for changes, surfaces what matters, and acts on your behalf, which leaves your team free to focus on what moves the business forward. Anyone can build agents, apps, and automations by describing what they need in plain language, without filing an IT ticket. Everything built in a shared space becomes an asset the whole team can use.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;h2 id="trust-built-in"&gt;Trust built in&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick is integrated with the security and compliance tools you already use, including Microsoft Purview for data protection, AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), and Microsoft Active Directory for permission-based controls. Quick reads the sensitivity labels already on your files, enforces existing permissions, and flags potentially sensitive data to administrators. Assets including agents, knowledge bases, and skills reach new teams only through approved sharing, and every approval and denial is recorded with who decided, what they shared, and why. Quick also carries the compliance certifications most organizations require, including HIPAA, FedRAMP, SOC 2, and ISO 27001. Your data is yours, and your conversations are private. No one else can browse your conversations, and IT admins cannot access your chat transcripts. Amazon CloudWatch and AWS CloudTrail are built in, giving your organization a full audit trail of what happened, who did it, and when. Your data never leaves your environment and never trains someone else’s models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That commitment is also why we’re now AWS Quick, a name that signals the enterprise security and governance that have been part of Quick from the start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="every-decision-stays-yours"&gt;Every decision stays yours&lt;/h2&gt;

You can &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/download/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;download Quick today and get started in minutes&lt;/a&gt;, or talk to your AWS account team about options for enterprise pricing plans.&lt;/p&gt;
