&lt;h2 id="explainable-vlm-content-evaluation-with-shap"&gt;Explainable VLM content evaluation with SHAP&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A quality label alone doesn’t tell selling partners what to fix first. To find which dimensions drove each decision, we use SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP), which assigns each feature a value showing how far it pushed a prediction toward high or low quality. Because the VLM and OCR scores feed the same classifier, SHAP measures both on a common scale. Raw scores can’t do this: a Visual Storytelling score of 6 and a high Character Density don’t tell you which one mattered more to the decision.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across the holdout set, the most influential features were OCR Character Density (mean absolute SHAP value 1.1), OCR Overall Readability (1.0), and VLM Visual Storytelling (1.0). They were followed by A+ Layout (0.8), Feature Presentation (0.6), and Conversion Optimization (0.4). Both OCR and VLM features rank near the top, so the classifier depends on both sources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-3.png" alt="Global SHAP value distributions across all VLM and OCR features" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Global SHAP value distributions for all VLM and OCR features&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For an individual page, SHAP shows which features lowered the decision. For each page classified as low quality, we rank the eight features by SHAP value, then pass the most negative features, the product category, and ecommerce best practices, along with the VLM’s rationales, to an LLM, which turns them into prioritized, specific actions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 4 shows two local SHAP plots with the LLM reasoning grounded in the SHAP results, for a low-quality and a high-quality A+ Content sample. For example, if A+ Layout has a SHAP value of -1.2 and Visual Storytelling has -0.3, the system prioritizes restructuring the layout and rebalancing text and images, followed by improvements to the imagery.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table style="border: 0;border-collapse: collapse;margin: 0 auto;width: 100%"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="border: 0;padding: 0 4px;text-align: center;width: 50%"&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-4.png" alt="Local SHAP distribution for a low-quality A+ Content sample where most features contribute negatively" width="390"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="border: 0;padding: 0 4px;text-align: center;width: 50%"&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-5.png" alt="Local SHAP distribution for a high-quality A+ Content sample where most features contribute positively" width="390"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 4: Two local SHAP value distributions for all VLM and OCR features: (a) a low-quality case, where most features contribute negatively and drive an improvement-requested recommendation; (b) a high-quality case, where most features contribute positively&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We validated the effectiveness of these explanation-based recommendations by measuring quality scores after selling partners revised their content. The average post-revision quality score increased by 1.4 points on a 10-point scale on a statistically significant sample. SHAP contributions can therefore be translated from decision explanations into actionable improvement priorities and methods. Explainability is therefore not merely an auxiliary capability for understanding the model. It functions as a feedback mechanism that materially improves content quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The complete system scores each page snapshot with the VLM and OCR, combines the four VLM scores and four OCR scores into a single feature vector, and passes it to a CatBoost classifier trained on expert labels. SHAP then identifies which features drove each decision. To measure what each component adds, we compared four configurations on the same 20 percent holdout set. The holdout set comes from 5,478 product detail pages across 10 product categories, including Furniture, Shoes, Grocery, Beauty, and Apparel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The baseline averages the four VLM scores and applies a fixed threshold, so every dimension counts equally. It reached 0.80 accuracy and an F1 score of 0.84. Training a CatBoost classifier on the same scores lets the model learn which dimensions matter most and how they interact, which raised accuracy to 0.89 and F1 to 0.90. A classifier trained on the OCR scores alone reached 0.82 accuracy and an F1 score of 0.83. Combining the VLM and OCR scores gave the best results, with 0.94 accuracy and an F1 score of 0.95. That’s 14 percentage points higher accuracy and 11 points higher F1 than the baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-6.png" alt="Bar chart comparing accuracy and F1 across VLM and OCR evaluation configurations" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Accuracy and F1 comparison across VLM and OCR evaluation configurations&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Examining each performance gain separately makes the value of the integrated approach clearer. CatBoost calibration first reduces the limitations of a fixed threshold by learning dimension-specific weights and nonlinear decision boundaries. Adding OCR features then supplies text-readability signals that the VLM can miss. Overall, the final fusion model improves accuracy by 0.14 and F1 by 0.11 over the baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We also changed the VLM backbone in an additional experiment on a single-category dataset containing 499 content projects. With a lighter VLM variant, the baseline achieved 0.74 accuracy and a 0.72 F1 score, while the &lt;code&gt;OCR-VLM-CatBoost&lt;/code&gt; model achieved 0.90 for both metrics. With a more capable VLM variant, the baseline achieved 0.79 accuracy and a 0.84 F1 score, while the fusion model achieved 0.94 and 0.95. The fusion approach improved accuracy by 0.16 and 0.15 for the two VLMs, respectively, demonstrating that complementary OCR features deliver consistent gains without depending on a particular foundation model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To reduce development costs while retaining access to high-performance foundation models, the system freezes the VLM and trains only the low-overhead classifier. In production, a worldwide A/B experiment that adjusted the visibility of low-quality A+ Content based on the model’s quality decisions produced a statistically meaningful improvement in Ordered Product Sales (OPS). Beyond aligning closely with expert judgment, the integrated system provides a quality signal that can support customer-experience and business decisions at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai"&gt;Implementation on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section describes how the architecture is implemented end-to-end on Amazon SageMaker AI. Each stage of the pipeline from content rendering and expert annotation through VLM scoring, OCR extraction, feature aggregation, classifier training, SHAP-based explainability, and production monitoring maps to a managed SageMaker AI capability. This design keeps the operational overhead low. The following steps walk through how each component is deployed, connected, and maintained in production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/30/ML-20598-7.png" alt="End-to-end architecture of the content-quality system on Amazon SageMaker AI" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Architecture of the content-quality system on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Content snapshot rendering and data ingestion:&lt;/strong&gt; The complete A+ Content experience is rendered, capturing how customers actually see the page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/processing-job.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Processing&lt;/a&gt; Jobs run containerized rendering jobs at scale, decoupling data preparation from training and inference compute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. VLM scoring and model hosting:&lt;/strong&gt; Annotators independently label content using the quality rubric. Their aggregated consensus labels become the training targets. The frozen VLM receives each snapshot plus a rubric prompt and outputs four scores from 0 to 10 (Visual Storytelling, Feature Presentation, A+ Layout, Conversion Optimization), each with a confidence value and rationale. SageMaker AI Inference hosts the VLM with the deployment option that fits the workload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Real-Time Endpoints for synchronous, low-latency scoring of individual listings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Asynchronous Endpoints for large payloads such as high-resolution snapshots, where a slight delay is acceptable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Batch Transform for offline bulk scoring at catalog scale.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These options support automatic scaling, managed infrastructure, and GPU instance selection.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/how-it-works-training.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Training Jobs&lt;/a&gt; can optionally be used to further tune or distill the VLM (for example, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) adapters, knowledge distillation into a smaller model) if teams want a specialized, lower-latency variant. Avoid this to keep recalibration cost-effective and VLM-agnostic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. OCR readability extraction:&lt;/strong&gt; In parallel, OCR extracts text and bounding-box positions from each snapshot and computes four readability signals: Text Density, Character Density, Spacing, and Overall Readability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt; provides pre-trained, single-action deployable models from its model hub, avoiding the need to source, package, and benchmark models from scratch. Teams select a model, optionally fine-tune it without managing infrastructure using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/customize-model.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Serverless Model Customization&lt;/a&gt; on ecommerce content, and deploy directly. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/deploy-model.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI Inference&lt;/a&gt; then hosts the chosen OCR model as an endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;4. Feature aggregation and storage:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/feature-store.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Feature Store&lt;/a&gt; provides both online (low-latency inference) and offline (batch training) access to the same features, avoiding training-serving skew. Teams can share features across the organization, so downstream consumers can reuse the same curated features.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;5. Classifier training:&lt;/strong&gt; A CatBoost classifier is trained on the eight-dimensional feature vector against expert annotations to learn non-uniform dimension weights and nonlinear decision boundaries. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/how-it-works-training.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Training Jobs&lt;/a&gt; can be used here again. You can register trained models directly to SageMaker Model Registry for versioning, approval workflows, and production deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;6. SHAP explainability and recommendations:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/processing-job.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Processing&lt;/a&gt; Jobs support &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/processing-container-run-scripts.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bring Your Own (BYO) processing code&lt;/a&gt;, which you can use to derive SHAP values and model explainability metrics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;7. Production monitoring and re-training:&lt;/strong&gt; In production, ML models naturally degrade over time. Customer aesthetics shift, new A+ module types launch, product categories evolve, and seller behavior changes. The scoring patterns the classifier learned from historical annotations gradually fall out of alignment with what quality means today, leading to silent accuracy erosion if left unchecked. Continuous monitoring closes this gap by detecting when the live data distribution or model predictions have drifted far enough from the training baseline to warrant recalibration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI ModelOps provides a &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-aiops-on-amazon-sagemakerai/tree/main" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;collection of examples&lt;/a&gt; and resources to help you operationalize generative AI and machine learning workloads on SageMaker AI.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-aiops-on-amazon-sagemakerai/tree/main" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AIOps&lt;/a&gt; provides the tool to track data quality, model quality, and feature-attribution drift. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/pipelines.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Pipelines&lt;/a&gt; also automates the re-training workflow end-to-end when drift thresholds are breached.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;8. Diffusion-based content generation:&lt;/strong&gt; Closing the loop, the quality scores and SHAP-derived recommendations condition a diffusion model that generates improved A+ image assets. Rather than leaving sellers to interpret recommendations and create new content from scratch, the system produces ready-to-use visual assets (upgraded lifestyle imagery, rebalanced layouts, or enhanced product shots) that directly address the dimensions SHAP flagged as most negatively contributing. These generated assets and natural-language improvement suggestions are surfaced to sellers, so they can upgrade their listings on amazon.com.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to evaluate product detail pages as customers see them by rendering each page as a single snapshot and scoring it with a VLM. We also walked through implementing the system on Amazon SageMaker AI. We built the system around three goals. For adaptability, we keep the VLM fixed and train a CatBoost classifier on its rubric scores, so the system matches expert judgment and can be updated with a short CPU-only job. For performance, we add OCR readability scores that catch dense or poorly spaced text the VLM misses. For explainability, SHAP identifies which features drove each decision, and an LLM turns those results into prioritized recommendations for selling partners.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On 5,478 product detail pages from 10 product categories, the fused model reached 0.94 accuracy and a 0.95 F1 score, 14 and 11 percentage points above the averaged-score baseline. Pages revised using the recommendations improved their quality score by an average of 1.4 points on a scale of 10.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Content quality standards differ across product categories, languages, and marketplaces, and our expert annotations can’t fully represent every context. Next, we plan to evaluate more efficient VLM backbones and inference methods to run the system on a global catalog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Use decision models with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/use-decision-models-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-evaluations/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sungyeon Kim]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 18:46:36 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">af187d4dcc140f40d628b5b7a009df4d46179afa</guid>

					<description>Some agent-evaluation questions are narrow and repeatable, and decision models answer exactly that kind of question. This post shows how to connect two decision models, Jev and Strands Decider, to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations through code-based evaluators, and how to validate a decision-model check before you gate a release on it.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Some of the questions you ask when evaluating an agent are narrow and repeatable. Did this response stay on policy? Is this tool call safe to approve? Does this answer match the retrieved context? Decision models answer exactly that kind of question. They take a structured state and a fixed set of allowed answers and return a calibrated probability over them, rather than free-form text whose confidence drifts from run to run. That makes them a strong fit for the scoring step in an agent evaluation, where you want a label and a number you can threshold and trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, scores an agent through its entire lifecycle, on demand during development, in batch jobs against stored sessions, and online against production traffic. Alongside 15 built-in evaluators, AgentCore Evaluations supports custom evaluators that you define as either an LLM-as-a-judge or a code-based evaluator. Custom LLM-as-a-judge evaluators apply your instructions and rating scale with a model in Amazon Bedrock. Code-based evaluators run your own logic in an AWS Lambda function, and that logic can call your choice of external system, model, or API, including a decision model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decision models suit questions with a fixed set of answers that an informed person could decide at a glance, such as whether the agent refused a request or whether a response stayed on topic. After you validate a question on your own traffic, a decision model can be sufficient for that decision or score, and you can extend the same approach to specific harmfulness or stereotyping categories in your policy. Because both backends can answer several questions about the same state in a single request, you can use them as a screening layer rather than a replacement, running a pass or fail signal across your metrics to select the turns that warrant deeper assessment by the built-in evaluators, whose explanations help you investigate what went wrong.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how to connect two decision model backends to AgentCore Evaluations through code-based evaluators. The first is &lt;a href="https://typesafe.ai/blog/introducing-system-one-models-and-jev" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Jev&lt;/a&gt;, a hosted decision model from TypeSafe. The second is &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/blog/introducing-strands-decider/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Decider 2B&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source decision model you run yourself. By the end of this post, you will know how to convert the AgentCore evaluator input into a decision model request, and the model’s probabilities back into an AgentCore evaluation result. First, though, when should you reach for a custom LLM-as-a-judge evaluator, and when does a decision model fit better? The next section breaks down what a decision model is and the trade-offs involved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decision-models"&gt;Decision models&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A decision model is a small, specialized model trained to select from a fixed set of options and return calibrated confidence scores rather than generating free-form text. A general-purpose large language model (LLM) evaluator produces a score with a rationale. A decision model instead produces a probability distribution over predefined answers. That gives you a direct confidence measure you can threshold, aggregate, and act on programmatically. This makes decision models well-suited for evaluation tasks where the judgment can be stated as a choice among a fixed set of options: “Is this response grounded?” or “Did the agent complete the user’s goal?”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decision models are most useful when you want a well-calibrated confidence score alongside the label, not just a binary judgment. They are also appropriate when you want to enforce a specific rubric consistently without relying on a general-purpose LLM to interpret it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Jev, the hosted decision model from TypeSafe, and &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/blog/introducing-strands-decider/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Decider&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source, 2-billion-parameter decision model from the Strands Agents team, both support three types of questions. A &lt;code&gt;noul&lt;/code&gt; question poses a yes/no question and returns a probability between 0 (no) and 1 (yes). A &lt;code&gt;choice&lt;/code&gt; question asks which of up to 255 options applies, returning the most probable option along with the full distribution and a confidence. A &lt;code&gt;score&lt;/code&gt; question asks which of 2 to 10 ordered levels applies, returning a probability-weighted score, the distribution over levels, and a confidence. Many evaluation rubrics fit one of these types. “Is every fact in this response supported by the tool results?” is a yes/no question, and “How helpful was this response, on a four-level rubric?” is a score.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decision-models-versus-llm-as-a-judge"&gt;Decision models versus LLM-as-a-judge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The built-in evaluators in AgentCore today use a general-purpose LLM to assess the agent response against a rubric and explain its judgment. This is valuable when the evaluation requires connecting evidence, identifying missing information, or working through a response in depth in tasks where the explanation is as useful as the score. Decision models are not a replacement for LLM evaluators. They’re an addition that the code-based evaluator framework makes possible. Use them for narrow, well-defined checks alongside the built-in evaluators’ broader assessment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To show how these evaluator types work together, this post builds an end-to-end example of an HR Assistant agent evaluated by deterministic code-based checks, built-in LLM-as-a-judge evaluators, and decision-model evaluators backed by Jev or Strands Decider 2B.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent is built with Strands Agents and deployed to AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. It answers employee questions using five tools that return fixed mock data such as paid time off (PTO) balances, PTO requests, HR policies, benefits, and pay stubs. We use fixed data in this code sample to make it straightforward to verify evaluator behavior from LLM-as-a-judge and decision models. When you additionally have ground truth, you can confirm whether each evaluator catches the right failures. This code sample helps you set up the framework for testing decision models compared to LLM-as-a-judge for your own use cases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-1.png" alt="Architecture of the HR Assistant agent on AgentCore runtime, evaluated by code-based, built-in, and decision-model evaluators" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: End-to-end architecture of the HR Assistant evaluated by code-based checks, built-in evaluators, and decision-model evaluators backed by Jev or Strands Decider&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy the sample, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account in a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Region&lt;/a&gt; where AgentCore Evaluations is available. The sample defaults to US West (Oregon).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-get-started.html#enabling-transaction-search" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Transaction Search enabled&lt;/a&gt; in the account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-access.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Access to a foundation model in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.12 or later, the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), and Docker.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Jev requires a TypeSafe API key. Visit &lt;a href="https://typesafe.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;typesafe.ai&lt;/a&gt; for access details.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Strands Decider requires a host running the Strands Decider server. You can run it locally for testing, or on Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) inside your virtual private cloud (VPC) so the Lambda function can reach it.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="seeing-it-in-action"&gt;Seeing it in action&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can find the full code sample in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/06-observe-evaluate-optimize-your-agent/02-evaluate/custom-code-based-evaluation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore samples GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. Two Python scripts handle everything:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;../utils/deploy.py&lt;/code&gt; deploys the HR Assistant to AgentCore runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;evaluate.py&lt;/code&gt; deploys evaluators, invokes the agent, and runs the evaluation modes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Running &lt;code&gt;evaluate.py&lt;/code&gt; without flags executes five steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Creates the Lambda execution role (&lt;code&gt;AgentCoreLambdaEvaluatorRole&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Packages and deploys two deterministic Lambda evaluators: &lt;code&gt;hr-response-length&lt;/code&gt; (a trace-level evaluator that checks whether the agent’s response stays within length bounds) and &lt;code&gt;hr-fact-checker&lt;/code&gt; (a session-level evaluator that regex-validates HR facts against the known mock data).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Registers both as AgentCore code-based evaluators through the control plane.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Runs on-demand evaluation with &lt;code&gt;EvaluationClient&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;OnDemandEvaluationDatasetRunner&lt;/code&gt; across five labeled scenarios, combining the code-based evaluators with &lt;code&gt;Builtin.Correctness&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Builtin.Helpfulness&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;Builtin.ResponseRelevance&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Creates an online evaluation configuration that continuously scores live sessions at 100 percent sampling.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At this point you have a working evaluation pipeline with deterministic checks and LLM-as-a-judge scoring. Decision-model evaluators are opt-in. You can add them with a flag, and they run alongside the base evaluators with no changes to the existing flow. Using the flags &lt;code&gt;--with-jev --jev-secret-arn &amp;lt;ARN&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; deploys &lt;code&gt;jev-evaluator&lt;/code&gt; and registers three evaluators: &lt;code&gt;JevGroundedness&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;JevHelpfulness&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;JevGoalCompletion&lt;/code&gt;. Using the flag &lt;code&gt;--with-decider&lt;/code&gt; deploys &lt;code&gt;strands-decider-evaluator&lt;/code&gt; and registers the same three evaluators backed by Strands Decider 2B. Add &lt;code&gt;--decider-ec2&lt;/code&gt; to auto-provision the Amazon EC2 instance, security groups, IAM role, and Lambda VPC configuration without manual console steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-2.png" alt="The evaluation pipeline that evaluate.py deploys, combining deterministic, built-in, and decision-model evaluators across on-demand and online modes" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The evaluation pipeline deployed by evaluate.py, from deterministic checks through built-in and decision-model evaluators&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each decision-model backend contributes three evaluators:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluator&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Question type&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Label&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;JevGroundedness / DeciderGroundedness&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TRACE&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;noul&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Grounded or Ungrounded&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;P(grounded)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;JevHelpfulness / DeciderHelpfulness&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TRACE&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;score&lt;/code&gt; (4-level)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Most probable level&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Expected level, scaled 0–1&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;JevGoalCompletion / DeciderGoalCompletion&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SESSION&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;choice&lt;/code&gt; (3-way)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Most probable option&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Probability-weighted value&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="build-the-evaluator-with-decision-models"&gt;Build the evaluator with decision models&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using the flags &lt;code&gt;--with-jev&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;--with-decider&lt;/code&gt; creates a custom code-based evaluator, where you have complete control of the code you want to run as your evaluator. We create AWS Lambda functions where the decision model is invoked inside an AWS Lambda function and returns a label or a numeric value. This Lambda function is registered as the code-based evaluator in AgentCore Evaluations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Lambda that is registered as the evaluator receives OpenTelemetry spans of the agent. The &lt;code&gt;spans.py&lt;/code&gt; module normalizes the shapes OpenTelemetry content can arrive in and then formats it appropriately to make the call to the decision model. For trace-level evaluation, only the targeted turn and brief prior history are sent, whereas the full conversation is included for session-level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-3.png" alt="The code-based evaluator Lambda converting OpenTelemetry spans into a decision-model request and mapping the probabilities back to an AgentCore result" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: How the code-based evaluator Lambda adapts OpenTelemetry spans for the decision model and maps the result back to AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The adapter converts the model’s probability distribution into a result that AgentCore evaluation expects. For a &lt;code&gt;noul&lt;/code&gt; question, the probability of &lt;code&gt;true&lt;/code&gt; becomes the value and the most probable answer becomes the label. For a &lt;code&gt;score&lt;/code&gt; question, the value is the probability-weighted expected level scaled to &lt;code&gt;[0, 1]&lt;/code&gt;. For a &lt;code&gt;choice&lt;/code&gt; question, it is the weighted average of predefined option values.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="validate-a-decision-model-evaluator-on-your-own-traffic"&gt;Validate a decision-model evaluator on your own traffic&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you rewrite a rubric as a decision model question, you’ve created a new measurement rather than a copy of the old one. A reframed rubric often lands stricter or looser than the original intended, and you won’t see that drift until you check it against real sessions. Before you gate a release on a decision-model evaluator, validate it the same way you’d validate any new measurement, against a labeled sample of your own traffic. The goal of this section is to walk you through that validation end to end, so you finish with an evaluator you can threshold with confidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The procedure has five steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1.&lt;/strong&gt; Build a labeled sample. Pull a set of stored sessions from your agent’s traffic, with enough coverage of the failures and edge cases you care about. A sample that covers only the happy path tells you nothing about where the evaluator breaks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2.&lt;/strong&gt; Get human reference labels. Have three independent labelers score each turn for the property your question measures and take the majority vote as the reference label. Keep track of which turns were unanimous and which were split. Although unanimous turns are the clear-cut cases, the split-vote turns are the valuable ones, because they reveal ambiguity in the rubric itself before the model ever sees it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3.&lt;/strong&gt; Run both evaluators on the same inputs. Run the decision-model evaluator and the matching built-in evaluator over those same sessions in a single batch evaluation. Scoring identical inputs is what makes the comparison apples to apples.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 4.&lt;/strong&gt; Read the results through three lenses. With the majority-vote labels and the easy and hard split in hand, you can now answer the three questions that tell you whether the evaluator is ready.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agreement rate: does it match your humans? Measure how often each evaluator agrees with the majority-vote labels, and do it per question, since one question’s accuracy says nothing about another’s. Then break the numbers out by easy (unanimous) and hard (split-vote) cases. An evaluator that stumbles only on the hard cases is behaving a lot like your human labelers did, which is exactly what you want.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Calibration: do the confidence scores mean something? Group turns by the evaluator’s reported probability and compare each group’s agreement with the labels. Higher confidence should track higher accuracy. Then check whether easy cases consistently draw higher confidence than hard ones. If they do, the model’s uncertainty is tracking genuine ambiguity, and you can use those groups to pick a decision threshold per evaluator instead of defaulting to 0.5.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Disagreement analysis: where and why do they differ? Find the turns where the two evaluators disagree with each other, and check whether those are the same turns your humans found ambiguous. Read them. Most disagreements trace back to the wording of the question, and a revised question clears them up. When the errors cluster in the hard cases, the rubric is the problem rather than the model.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 5&lt;/strong&gt;. Re-run the check when anything changes. Treat this as a repeatable check rather than a one-time gate. Any time you reword a question or move to a new model version, run it again. Each result’s explanation records the model version that produced it, so you know which version a given score came from.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choosing-the-best-set-of-evaluators-for-your-agent"&gt;Choosing the best set of evaluators for your agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The built-in evaluators remain the foundation of broad evaluation strategy in AgentCore Evaluations. They assess the response against a rubric and its supporting context, and explain the judgment so developers can investigate what went wrong. For the HR Assistant, a faithfulness evaluation can identify a claim that contradicts a tool result, whereas a helpfulness evaluation can point out that the agent answered a balance question but left a requested action incomplete.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Faithfulness, helpfulness, and correctness can require connecting evidence, identifying missing information, or working through a solution. The preprint &lt;em&gt;JEV-as-a-Judge: Accept When Confident, Escalate When Unsure&lt;/em&gt; describes a related distinction as “reading versus deriving.” A narrow decision-model check can flag a turn for attention, whereas the built-in evaluator provides the broader assessment and reasoning needed to review it. Decision models shine brightest when you treat them as a gate rather than a replacement. The cascade pattern works like this. First, run a fast, calibrated decision model on every turn of every session to get a cheap pass/fail signal. Then invoke a full LLM evaluator only on sessions where the signal falls below your threshold.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22136-4.png" alt="The cascade pattern, where a decision model screens every turn and escalates low-confidence turns to a full LLM evaluator" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The cascade pattern, where a calibrated decision model screens every turn and only low-confidence turns escalate to a built-in evaluator&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Criterion&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;LLM as Judge&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decision Models as Judge&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Output&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Score + explanation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Calibrated probability&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2–10 s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~hundreds of ms (network)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customizable&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Limited (fixed prompt templates)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Question definitions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Best for&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Qualitative nuance, explanation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Calibrated judgment&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We recommend the following setup:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure online evaluation with the built-in evaluators you rely on, as the standard measure of quality across your agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add decision-model evaluators for domain-specific yes/no and choice questions to the same configuration, along with deterministic code-based evaluators for anything you can compute exactly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Each week, or on whatever schedule suits your release cycle, query the online results for sessions where a decision-model check failed or scored near its threshold, and run batch evaluations on those sessions with the built-in evaluators to get explanations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Gate releases on the built-in evaluators and on the decision-model evaluators you have validated against labeled data, and track how often the two agree.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, run the &lt;code&gt;cleanup.py&lt;/code&gt; that deletes the agent hosted on AgentCore runtime, the evaluators and evaluation config, the AWS Lambda functions that are registered as the evaluators, the EC2 instance that hosts the Strands Decider model, AWS Secrets Manager key and the associated log group on CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you connected two decision models, Jev and Strands Decider, to AgentCore Evaluations using code-based evaluators, and the accompanying code sample gives you a framework for testing those models and seeing where they add value as evaluators. The built-in evaluators remain your baseline. Decision models add narrow, validated checks that come with a measure of certainty you can threshold and act on. The practical path forward is to add decision-model checks alongside the built-in evaluators you already run, measure how often they agree with your reference labels on your own traffic, and expand coverage only once you trust them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, review the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt; documentation, integrate custom evaluators using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-custom-code-based-evaluators-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;code-based evaluators&lt;/a&gt; and take a deeper look into &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/blog/introducing-strands-decider/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Decider&lt;/a&gt; model. Get started with code samples by deploying the sample evaluators from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore sample repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Smart Approvals in AWS Quick: Human oversight where it matters</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/smart-approvals-in-amazon-quick-human-oversight-where-it-matters/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Steve Vinsik]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 16:33:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Thought Leadership]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">9c5c128f7a31e5e43f3eafba7ef1c3537f53e875</guid>

					<description>Smart Approvals in Amazon Quick reviews eligible agent actions within your existing controls, reducing unnecessary approval prompts while keeping consequential decisions with a person. See how policy sets the boundary, how a classifier reviews actions, and how AWS CloudTrail and Amazon CloudWatch Logs support audit.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;How should security leaders preserve human oversight as AI agents take on more work? Permissions establish which actions agents can take, but a permitted action can still warrant a person’s judgment. Requiring approval for every step might seem like the safest approach, yet multi-step tasks can produce &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.05307" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repeated prompts&lt;/a&gt;, even for routine actions. &lt;a href="https://doi.org/10.25300/MISQ/2018/14124" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Research on security warnings&lt;/a&gt; shows that attention and adherence can decline with repetition. The challenge isn’t to require more approvals, but to preserve attention for decisions that need human review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Smart Approvals in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt; addresses this challenge by reviewing eligible actions within your existing controls. It evaluates each proposed action against the user’s request and either approves it automatically or returns it to the user for review. It does this without granting additional permissions or removing required human approvals. Security leaders retain control over which actions must always go to a person, while Smart Approvals is designed to reduce unnecessary interruptions in routine work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22232-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img title="Smart Approvals within policy boundaries" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22232-1.png" alt="Existing controls route proposed actions to required user approval, Smart Approvals review, or a policy block. Smart Approvals may approve an eligible action for its exact parameters or return it to the user. Assessment errors do not grant automatic approval. Permission update events and setting changes are recorded in AWS CloudTrail." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Policy determines eligibility. Smart Approvals approves eligible actions or returns them to the user for review. Policy denials remain blocked&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="policy-sets-the-boundary"&gt;Policy sets the boundary&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick evaluates each proposed action against Custom Permissions, Quick Guardrails, connector action permissions, and the user’s settings before automated review. Denied actions remain denied, and mandatory human approvals still apply. With Smart Approvals enabled, an action is eligible only when the user’s setting is “Ask each time” and no other control requires human approval. Connector action permissions, configured when a connector is created, can require human approval for specific actions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Administrators use Custom Permissions to determine who can use Smart Approvals. It’s on by default, but profiles with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/custom-permissions-governance.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deny-by-default&lt;/a&gt; enabled must explicitly allow it. Users can also turn Smart Approvals off for themselves. That choice applies across conversations and scheduled runs. When it’s off, actions otherwise eligible for automated review require the user’s approval, and no account-level setting can turn it back on for them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-eligible-actions-are-reviewed"&gt;How eligible actions are reviewed&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Smart Approvals uses a separate model, the classifier, to evaluate eligible actions against the user’s request and a bounded view of the conversation. It considers what the action does, where data goes, and who it affects. It can consult the user’s knowledge graph, which contains people and working relationships drawn from connected sources. This helps it understand how recipients relate to the user, such as whether a recipient is in the user’s reporting chain. This lookup stays within your Quick account. Quick doesn’t use the content the classifier reviews, including knowledge graph data, to train models or improve the service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An action can match the request and still warrant human review. Examples include sending content outside the organization or to senior leaders in the user’s reporting chain, changing access, deleting records, or initiating payments. This list isn’t complete, and the classifier also weighs the surrounding context. Reviewing actions against the user’s request adds a check against indirect prompt injection, where instructions in retrieved content attempt to redirect the agent. Quick instructs both the agent and classifier to treat that content as information, not instructions. Model review can still make mistakes, so actions that must always receive human approval, including those that move sensitive data, should require it through connector action permissions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The classifier provides judgment. The permission service enforces authority. Each automatic approval is single-use, applies only to the exact action and parameters reviewed, and grants no ongoing permission. Quick labels automatic approvals in the conversation. Actions the classifier doesn’t approve return to the user, as do timeouts and other review failures, which never grant automatic approval. These enforcement boundaries limit what an approval authorizes. They don’t establish that the classifier’s judgment was correct.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="when-an-action-goes-beyond-the-request"&gt;When an action goes beyond the request&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An account manager asks Quick to prepare a quarterly business review brief for AnyCompany using her team’s account notes and last quarter’s email. She then asks Quick to send it to two colleagues she names. Assuming the send is eligible for automated review, Smart Approvals evaluates the proposed action against her request and the available conversation context.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now suppose an account note says the brief should also go to an all-sales distribution list of about 2,000 people. That would change the audience beyond the recipients she specified, so Smart Approvals is designed to return the proposed send to her for review, where she can decline it. An approval to send to the original recipients can’t be reused for the wider audience.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="audit-evidence-and-investigation"&gt;Audit evidence and investigation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Quick permission service records permission updates associated with Smart Approvals in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudTrail&lt;/a&gt; in your account. Each event identifies the user, connector, action, and time, and marks the request as associated with Smart Approvals. That marker doesn’t establish that the permission service accepted the grant or that the action ran. Quick also records permission-check verdicts, including denials from connector action permissions, and changes to a user’s Smart Approvals setting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Investigators can correlate these events by user and time with other Quick activity in CloudTrail and logs delivered through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Logs&lt;/a&gt;. Chat logs capture the user’s request, system response, and conversation context. Agent metadata logs record changes to agents. Records from the destination system, such as a sent email or updated ticket, confirm whether the action completed. Together, these sources help reconstruct what happened without treating approval as proof of execution. CloudTrail Event history retains events for 90 days, so configure a trail for longer investigations. Because chat logs can contain sensitive content, restrict access and consider CloudWatch Logs data protection policies to mask sensitive data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-to-start"&gt;Where to start&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because Smart Approvals is on by default, begin by confirming that actions requiring human approval are configured through connector action permissions. Set up a CloudTrail trail and delivery of chat logs and agent metadata logs to CloudWatch Logs to support investigations. Chat logs capture only activity after delivery is configured, so earlier approvals will lack that conversation context. With approval requirements and logging in place, use Custom Permissions to limit Smart Approvals to a pilot team working on a defined workflow before extending it to other teams.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before the pilot, define what success looks like: fewer prompts for routine actions, with consequential actions still returning to a person for review. During the pilot, review a sample of automatic approvals and test high-impact cases, such as sending content outside the organization or adding a recipient introduced by a document. Confirm that these cases come back to the user. Confirm that your team can trace each action from approval through execution. Expand to more teams and workflows only when the results meet those criteria, and reassess as actions and policies change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The measure of effective oversight isn’t how often an agent asks for approval, but whether people retain control over consequential decisions. For security leaders, the case for expanding Smart Approvals should rest on evidence from the pilot. Fewer prompts are valuable when they preserve attention without weakening accountability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For setup steps, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/smart-approvals.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Smart Approvals&lt;/a&gt; in the governance section of the AWS Quick User Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;About the author&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;footer&gt; 
 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt; 
  &lt;div class="blog-author-image" style="margin-right: 1.0em"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-22232-2.jpg" alt="Steve Vinsik" width="100" height="133"&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Steve Vinsik&lt;/h3&gt; 
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Steve is a Principal Product Manager at AWS, where he leads governance, identity, and security for AWS Quick. He works directly with enterprise CIOs and CISOs to define the controls their organizations need to adopt AI agents at scale, such as access permissions, guardrails, model management, data loss prevention, and observability. Before joining Quick, Steve led product for AWS Identity, including AWS IAM Identity Center and AWS Builder ID, which he scaled from launch to millions of users. He has more than 25 years of experience delivering mission-critical security programs for government customers, with executive roles at General Dynamics, Unisys, and Safran.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt;
