		<item>
		<title>Four layers of context engineering for production AI agents: How Deep Insight applies them in working code</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/four-layers-of-context-engineering-for-production-ai-agents-how-deep-insight-applies-them-in-working-code/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Yoonseo Kim]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 16:30:40 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Strands Agents]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">f002aac1d7eda07b2b65eb10ab1188bfa0625420</guid>

					<description>In production AI agents, context management is the real bottleneck. This post presents a four-layer context engineering framework (architecture, prompts, file-based tools, and verification agents), implemented in working code from Deep Insight, an open source multi-agent data analysis solution on the Strands Agents SDK and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When production AI agents handle tasks such as data analysis and report generation, context management can become a major bottleneck. A 200K-token window sounds generous until a real-world task burns through most of it in minutes. Data analysis and report generation leave the agent failing mid-execution, losing accuracy, or driving up costs. A body of published guidance already addresses this, but the individual techniques are easier to read about than to apply. Applied together, they make a measurable difference: in the reference implementation this post walks through, a single write-and-execute tool cuts the tokens each script adds to context by about 95 percent, and loading skills on demand shrinks the system prompt by about 97 percent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This series organizes that guidance into practices you can apply directly. Anthropic’s engineering posts, together with features built into the Strands Agents SDK, converge on four layers of context engineering: isolate context at the architecture level, control it through prompts, externalize it to the filesystem, and verify it with independent agents and safeguards. In this post (Part 2), you will learn how each layer works and what it looks like in working code. We applied these techniques in &lt;em&gt;Deep Insight&lt;/em&gt;, an open source multi-agent data analysis solution built on the Strands Agents SDK and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, which serves as the reference implementation throughout.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="post-series"&gt;Post series&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/5-production-challenges-in-multi-agent-systems-and-how-deep-insight-solved-them/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1: 5 production challenges in multi-agent systems and how Deep Insight solved them.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Part 2: Four layers of context engineering for production AI agents: How Deep Insight applies them in working code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Part 3: Harness engineering in practice: moving Deep Insight from dev to production. (Coming soon!)&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first post defined five core challenges that production multi-agent systems must solve. It introduced how &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt; each address different layers of the problem, with code examples. This second post takes the four layers of context engineering practice that sit on top of that architecture and shows each one implemented. The third post guides you through deploying and operating agents in a secure private network on Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC), covering everything needed for real-world operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-is-context-engineering"&gt;What is context engineering?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Context engineering is the discipline of optimizing the information in a large language model (LLM)’s limited context window so agents perform at their best. Anthropic Claude Sonnet 4.5, available on Amazon Bedrock, offers a 200K context window, but complex production tasks quickly exceed it. For example, a seemingly straightforward request like “&lt;em&gt;Analyze sales data and create a DOCX report&lt;/em&gt;” runs through several stages: data loading and exploration (5 min), category-based sales analysis (3 min), generating five charts for time-series analysis (10 min), statistical validation (3 min), and final report generation (5 min). Together they consume over 150K tokens across 15 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Processing such tasks in a single context causes problems: the window overflows mid-task, stale context degrades performance, and errors require re-running the whole pipeline. As context grows, attention to important information drops, and inefficiency raises cost under per-token pricing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“As the number of tokens in the context window increases, the model’s ability to accurately recall information from that context decreases (Context rot).”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Anthropic, “&lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents&lt;/a&gt;” (2025.09)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Context engineering solves exactly these problems, and it is not only a collection of techniques. Spanning architecture design, prompt writing, tool implementation, and verification, it’s a layered discipline where each layer compensates for what the previous ones miss. This post details the four-layer approach you can apply to manage context efficiently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-to-handle-tasks-that-exceed-the-context-window"&gt;How to handle tasks that exceed the context window&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Anthropic has published several techniques for handling long-horizon tasks. Deep Insight groups these techniques into four layers, with each layer addressing the limitations of the others. The following table sets out the four layers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Practice&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Core Principle&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Limitations&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Layer 1: Architecture&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Isolating Context Through Multi-Agent Architecture&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Each agent operates in an independent context&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;When isolation alone is not enough&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Layer 2: Prompt&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Output Control and Behavioral Rules&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Limiting context intake through prompts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;When prompts alone are not enough&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Layer 3: Tools&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;File-based Context Externalization&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Keeping heavy content in files outside the context&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;When issues still arise&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Layer 4: Verification Agents and Safeguards&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Independent Verification and Safeguards&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Catching errors and preventing context overflow&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Final line of defense&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sections that follow take each layer in order, stating what the layer prescribes, why the preceding layers alone are insufficient, and how the practice is implemented in Deep Insight.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="isolating-context-through-multi-agent-architecture"&gt;1. Isolating context through multi-agent architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Give each specialized agent its own context window and pass only compressed results to the orchestrator. Deep Insight does this with a hierarchy of Coordinator, Planner, Plan Reviewer, Supervisor, and Tool Agents (Coder, Validator, Reporter, Tracker, Auditor).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With a single agent, all information, from user requests through data exploration, analysis, chart generation, validation, and reporting, accumulates in one context and easily exceeds the window. Giving each agent its own context keeps even the Coder, which uses the most, under 25K tokens total. Each agent receives only what it needs and signals others with a completion notice and brief summary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key to isolation is minimizing shared information. Deep Insight uses a Python dictionary, &lt;code&gt;shared_state&lt;/code&gt;, that stores only four things:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;messages&lt;/code&gt;: The previous agent’s final message (not the full conversation history).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;clues&lt;/code&gt;: Compressed summaries collected from each agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;full_plan&lt;/code&gt;: A checklist generated by Planner and updated by Tracker.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;history&lt;/code&gt;: A brief log of agent names and messages.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The clues use a fixed CLUES_FORMAT: instead of each agent’s full conversation (tens of thousands of tokens), only compressed core results are passed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# coder_agent_tool.py -- context passing format
CLUES_FORMAT = "Here is clues from {}:\n\n&amp;lt;clues&amp;gt;\n{}\n&amp;lt;/clues&amp;gt;\n\n"
RESPONSE_FORMAT = "Response from {}:\n\n&amp;lt;response&amp;gt;\n{}\n&amp;lt;/response&amp;gt;\n\n*Please execute the next step.*"

# After Coder completes: add only compressed result to clues
clues += CLUES_FORMAT.format("coder", response["text"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pattern is the one recommended in Anthropic’s “&lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How We Built Our Multi-Agent Research System&lt;/a&gt;” post, which describes an orchestrator-worker pattern in which a lead agent delegates to specialized subagents, with each agent working only within its own context. You do not need to build it yourself: the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/user-guide/concepts/multi-agent/agents-as-tools/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agents-as-tools&lt;/a&gt; pattern in the Strands Agents SDK provides this isolation at the framework level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="controlling-context-inflow-through-prompts"&gt;2. Controlling context inflow through prompts&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Layer 1 isolation alone is not sufficient: even with independent contexts, an agent that generates long responses inflates the Supervisor’s context. Layer 2 limits each agent’s output through prompts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Deep Insight, we use prompts to limit how much information each agent returns to the Supervisor. Three rules do most of the work, as Deep Insight’s prompts show.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Start with the strongest model and a simple prompt. Then incrementally add specific instructions based on actually observed failure cases.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Anthropic, “Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents” (2025.09)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-2-1-explicit-per-agent-output-token-budgets"&gt;Implementation detail 2-1: Explicit per-agent output token budgets&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each agent’s prompt specifies a maximum response token count, preventing long responses that pollute the Supervisor’s context.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Output Token Budget&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reason&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Coder&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,000–1,500 tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Analysis code is saved to files, so the agent returns only a summary of state, completed tasks, and insights&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validator&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;800 tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validation results are saved to &lt;code&gt;citations.json&lt;/code&gt;. The agent returns only a summary&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reporter&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,000 tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent generates the report as a DOCX file and returns only a summary of the result&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Anthropic’s ” &lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/writing-tools-for-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Writing Effective Tools for Agents&lt;/a&gt;” post suggests giving tools a CONCISE or DETAILED response option for context efficiency. Apply the same idea one level up, in each agent’s system prompt. In Deep Insight, all agents share one standardized format:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;## Status
[SUCCESS | PARTIAL_SUCCESS | ERROR]
## Completed Tasks
- [Task 1 from FULL_PLAN -- use exact wording so Tracker can match]
- [Task 2 -- be specific, not generic]
## Key Insights
- [Key finding 1 with numbers and ratios]
- [Key finding 2 with business implications]
## Generated Files
- ./artifacts/chart.png -- brief description
- ./artifacts/calculation_metadata.json -- N calculations&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-2-2-self-contained-code-philosophy"&gt;Implementation detail 2-2: Self-contained code philosophy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Coder agent’s prompt explicitly states “variables do not persist between scripts.” Every script must include all necessary imports and load data directly. The prompt provides both incorrect and correct examples:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# WRONG -- Attempting to use a variable from Turn 1 in Turn 2
# Turn 1:
df = pd.read_csv('data.csv')
total = df['Amount'].sum()

# Turn 2:
print(f"Total: {total}")  # NameError: name 'total' is not defined

# CORRECT -- Every script is self-contained
import pandas as pd
df = pd.read_csv('./artifacts/cache/df_main.csv')  # Load from cache
total = df['Amount'].sum()
print(f"Total: {total}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reason this rule relates to context engineering is that referencing variables from previous scripts requires the agent to “remember” prior conversations, which means past code must remain in context. The self-contained principle means past code can be safely removed from context.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-2-3-supervisors-section-completion-rule"&gt;Implementation detail 2-3: Supervisor’s section completion rule&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Supervisor’s prompt includes the rule “Do not move to the next agent until all checklist items in the current agent’s section are marked &lt;code&gt;[x]&lt;/code&gt;.” We express this rule in the prompt because deciding whether the Coder’s work is complete requires the Supervisor to interpret the plan using information from the Tracker’s updates. This judgment is difficult to express in code alone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="only-pointers-in-context-actual-data-in-files"&gt;3. “Only pointers in context, actual data in files”&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Layer 1 isolates context between agents, and Layer 2 limits output within agents. But when the Coder agent generates 100 lines of Python code, that code itself enters the context. Even with “respond briefly” prompt instructions, some code can’t be written briefly. Layer 3 uses tool design to move heavy content outside the context, into the filesystem. Four techniques carry most of the benefit, as the following implementation details show.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-3-1-moving-code-outside-context-with-write-and-execute-tool"&gt;Implementation detail 3-1: Moving code outside context with Write and Execute Tool&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Coder reads the analysis plan, writes Python, and executes it to generate visualizations. But when the code gets long, the conventional approach (separate file-write and execute tools) adds 100 lines directly to context per script, accumulating over 1,000 lines.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The fix is to collapse the two steps into a single tool, so the code never enters context as a message. Deep Insight’s Coder agent uses one &lt;code&gt;write_and_execute_tool&lt;/code&gt; for this.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here’s part of the write_and_execute_tool. After writing and executing the file, it returns only a summary: “Wrote 105 lines, execution successful, completed analysis of five categories.” The code stays in the file and about three lines of summary remain in context. Based on this example, that saves about 95 percent of the tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# write_and_execute_tool.py
def _handle_write_and_execute_tool(file_path, content, timeout=300):
    """Write a Python script and immediately execute it."""
    # Step 1: Write file
    with open(file_path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        f.write(content)
    # Step 2: Execute immediately
    result = subprocess.run(
        f"python {file_path}",
        shell=True,
        capture_output=True,
        timeout=timeout
    )
    # Return only summary
    return f"Written {len(content.split(chr(10)))} lines to {file_path}\n" \
        f"Execution successful\n" \
        f"Output: {result.stdout[:500]}"  # Only first 500 chars&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Server-side filtering: filtering data at the execution layer before it reaches context, 98.7% token reduction (150K to 2K).”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Anthropic, “&lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/code-execution-with-mcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Code Execution with MCP&lt;/a&gt;” (2025.11)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-3-2-reusing-common-logic-as-modules-in-the-coder-agent"&gt;Implementation detail 3-2: Reusing common logic as modules in the Coder agent&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Common modules get reused across analyses. Rather than regenerating them each time, define frequently-used functions as a module once and import them, which prevents token waste and context duplication.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Without this, defining functions like to_python_type() or track_calculation() in every analysis produces 200–300 lines of duplicate code across 10 analyses. Deep Insight generates a module called &lt;code&gt;coder_analysis_utils.py&lt;/code&gt; before the first task. It includes functions for converting numpy types, tracking calculation metadata, and saving to JSON. Subsequent analyses import it, replacing 20–30 regenerated lines with two import lines, which saves tokens and improves consistency and debugging.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# coder_analysis_utils.py -- common module generated before first task
import numpy as np
import json
from datetime import datetime

_calculations = []

def to_python_type(value):
    """Convert numpy/pandas types to Python native types"""
    if isinstance(value, (np.integer, np.int64)): return int(value)
    elif isinstance(value, (np.floating, np.float64)): return float(value)
    elif isinstance(value, np.ndarray): return value.tolist()
    return value

def track_calculation(calc_id, value, description, formula,
                      source_file, source_columns, importance):
    """Track calculation metadata -- Validator independently verifies later"""
    _calculations.append({
        "id": calc_id, "value": to_python_type(value),
        "description": description, "formula": formula,
        "source_file": source_file, "source_columns": source_columns,
        "importance": importance
    })

def save_calculation_metadata(path="./artifacts/calculation_metadata.json"):
    """Save all tracked calculations to JSON"""
    with open(path, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        json.dump({"generated_at": datetime.now().isoformat(),
                   "calculations": _calculations}, f, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-3-3-persisting-and-passing-work-products-through-structured-note-taking"&gt;Implementation detail 3-3: Saving and sharing analysis results through structured note-taking&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We use structured note-taking to share detailed analysis results without passing them through each agent’s context. Deep Insight stores these results in a file called &lt;code&gt;all_results.txt&lt;/code&gt;, which is then read by agents as needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, the Coder agent appends results to &lt;code&gt;all_results.txt&lt;/code&gt; after each analysis task:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;with open('./artifacts/all_results.txt', 'a', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    f.write(f"""
## Category Sales Analysis (completed at: 14:32)
- Top revenue category: Fruit (417,166,008 KRW, 45% of total)
- Top 3 categories: Fruit, Vegetables, Meat (78% of total)
- Generated file: ./artifacts/category_sales_chart.png
""")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Coder records detailed results in this file after each analysis, and the Reporter reads it when writing the final report. Other agents collaborate through all_results.txt the same way.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Without note-taking, the Coder passes detailed results to the Supervisor after every task, accumulating in its context. With note-taking, only an approximately 30-token message like “Category sales analysis complete, 3 key insights found, detailed results saved to all_results.txt” is passed, and the Reporter reads the file when needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern generalizes beyond a single file. The filesystem itself can serve as the inter-agent communication bus, as it does in Deep Insight:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;File name&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Producer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consumer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;all_results.txt&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Coder&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reporter&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cumulative log of analysis results&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;calculation_metadata.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Coder&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validator&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Record of numeric calculations: sources, formulas, and values (for verification)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;citations.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validator&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reporter&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Verified citation data (inserted as [1], [2] in the report)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;audit_findings.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Auditor&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reporter&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Citation defects found in the final report (drives targeted Reporter retries)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;coder_analysis_utils.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Coder&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;All scripts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Shared utility functions (write once, import repeatedly)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;full_plan (shared_state)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Planner/Tracker&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Supervisor&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Checklist status (updating [x])&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The”just in time” approach, dynamically loading data into context at runtime rather than pre-processing all relevant data up front, keeps context fresh, relevant, and adaptive to the evolving state of the task.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Anthropic, “&lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents&lt;/a&gt;” (2025.09)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-3-4-loading-only-needed-knowledge-with-skills"&gt;Implementation detail 3-4: Loading only needed knowledge with Skills&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rather than putting all specialized knowledge into the system prompt, this approach loads it only when needed. Including every skill guide (data structure checking, README generation, skill creation, system prompt writing, tool creation) would add over 20K tokens, load unnecessary information into the Coder agent on every call, and force a redeploy to add a skill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deep Insight’s Skills system loads only a skill catalog of about 500 tokens at startup, with the skill list holding only names and brief descriptions. When a user requests “Create a README file,” the agent decides “I need the readme-generator skill” and calls &lt;code&gt;skill_tool(skill_name="readme-generator")&lt;/code&gt;. The SkillLoader reads readme-generator/SKILL.md and returns a detailed guide of about 3K tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# 1. Skill Discovery (at initialization)
from src.utils.skills.skill_utils import initialize_skills
available_skills, skill_prompt = initialize_skills(
    skill_dirs=["./skills"],
    verbose=True
)

# 2. Add to System Prompt (~500-token catalog)
system_prompt = base_prompt + skill_prompt

# 3. Runtime -- Agent loads skill when needed
# Agent decides: "This is README generation, I need the readme-generator skill"
# -&amp;gt; skill_tool(skill_name="readme-generator")
# -&amp;gt; Returns full SKILL.md content (~3K tokens)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each skill is defined as a &lt;code&gt;SKILL.md&lt;/code&gt; file composed of YAML frontmatter and detailed markdown guide. It includes metadata like name, description, license, and allowed-tools, along with Overview, When to Use This Skill, detailed instructions, and Best Practices. To add a new skill, create a new directory under skills/ and write a SKILL.md file, and it is automatically discovered.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At startup, the agent loads a skill catalog of about 500 tokens instead of wasting over 20,000 tokens on full guides (about 97 percent less). When needed, it can load an additional few thousand tokens’ worth of detailed instructions for skills.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="verifying-quality-with-verification-agents-and-safeguards"&gt;4. Verifying quality with verification agents and safeguards&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Even with Layers 1–3, one risk remains: isolated agents can’t see each other’s work, so if the Coder miscalculates, the Reporter carries that error into the report. Layer 4 is the final line of defense.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Provide testing tools so the agent can self-verify features and fix bugs that aren’t obvious from the code alone. Have the model commit its progress to git with descriptive commit messages and write progress summaries in a progress file.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Anthropic, “&lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Effective Harnesses for Long-Running Agents&lt;/a&gt;” (2025.11)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-4-1-tracker-validator-and-auditor-agents-progress-tracking-and-quality-assurance"&gt;Implementation detail 4-1: Tracker, Validator, and Auditor agents: progress tracking and quality assurance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tracker updates the plan’s checklist in real time. When the Coder completes data loading, the Supervisor calls Tracker, which updates the plan item from &lt;code&gt;[ ]&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;[x]&lt;/code&gt; based on information like “Data load complete (1,000 rows, 5 columns).” This helps both users and the system know exactly where progress stands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Validator independently re-executes the Coder’s calculations to verify accuracy. It reads &lt;code&gt;calculation_metadata.json&lt;/code&gt;, re-performs each calculation from source data, and compares: matches within 0.01 pass, mismatches are flagged. Verified calculations go to &lt;code&gt;citations.json&lt;/code&gt; for the Reporter to cite. After the Reporter finishes, the Auditor checks the final DOCX against &lt;code&gt;citations.json&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;calculation_metadata.json&lt;/code&gt; without modifying them. It records citation defects in &lt;code&gt;audit_findings.json&lt;/code&gt; and can request up to two targeted retries from the Reporter.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-4-2-priority-based-verification-optimization"&gt;Implementation detail 4-2: Prioritizing calculations for verification&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Verifying every calculation is inefficient, so scope the verification by importance. Deep Insight’s OptimizedValidator applies the following thresholds:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Over 50 calculations: aggressive filtering, verify all high plus only 10 medium.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Over 20 calculations: moderate filtering, verify all high plus 15 medium.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;20 or fewer calculations: verify most, all high plus all medium plus 5 low.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It also applies type-safe comparison: to avoid numpy-versus-Python type issues, all values are converted to &lt;code&gt;float()&lt;/code&gt; and checked for an absolute difference under 0.01:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# validator.md -- type-safe numerical comparison
try:
    match = abs(float(expected) - float(actual)) &amp;lt; 0.01
except:
    match = str(expected) == str(actual)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-4-3-summarizingconversationmanager-the-last-safety-net"&gt;Implementation detail 4-3: SummarizingConversationManager: the last safety net&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Context can still overflow in exceptional cases, so keep a safety net under the other layers. Deep Insight uses the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/user-guide/concepts/agents/conversation-management/#summarizingconversationmanager" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SummarizingConversationManager&lt;/a&gt; in the Strands Agents SDK for this.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# strands_sdk_utils.py -- safety net configuration at agent creation
agent = Agent(
    model=llm,
    system_prompt=system_prompts,
    tools=tools,
    conversation_manager=SummarizingConversationManager(
        summary_ratio=0.5,  # summarize oldest 50% on overflow
        preserve_recent_messages=10,  # always preserve 10 most recent messages
        summarization_system_prompt=apply_prompt_template("summarization", {})
    )
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On overflow, the LLM summarizes the oldest 50 percent of messages into one, while always preserving the 10 most recent to keep the current task’s context. The summarization prompt (summarization.md) requires &lt;code&gt;clues&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;plans&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;tracking status&lt;/code&gt; to be preserved verbatim.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="implementation-detail-4-4-selective-prompt-caching-for-cost-optimization"&gt;Implementation detail 4-4: Selective prompt caching for cost optimization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Context engineering covers cost optimization, not only token savings. Enable prompt caching per agent, based on how often each is called with the same prompt. Deep Insight’s per-agent settings:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prompt Cache&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool Cache&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reason&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Supervisor&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;High frequency of repeated calls, with a large system prompt and tool definitions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Coder&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Running multiple analysis scripts sequentially, with the same prompt each time&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reporter&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Generating a report incrementally section by section, with high prompt-reuse frequency&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Auditor&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inspects the DOCX and JSON files over many turns with the same prompt and tools, and reruns after each Reporter retry&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Coordinator&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A single one-off call, no caching benefit&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Planner&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Called once per request, and again only when the user requests a plan revision&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Validator&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Called with different data each time, low cache hit rate&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tracker&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Lightweight task, no tools, caching unnecessary&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents with caching enabled insert cache points into system prompts and tool definitions, reducing input token costs by up to 90 percent on repeated calls. This is a feature natively supported by the Strands Agents SDK. Plan Reviewer does not appear in the table because it’s a human-in-the-loop step that collects user approval or feedback rather than calling a model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post has detailed the four-layer engineering approach you can use to efficiently manage context for complex tasks that exceed the context window.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Layer 1 (architecture-level isolation): Each agent works in an independent context through multi-agent architecture, passing only compressed summaries.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Layer 2 (prompt-based control): Output token budgets, standardized response formats, and self-contained code philosophy actively limit context inflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Layer 3 (filesystem use): write_and_execute_tool keeps code outside context, the filesystem serves as an inter-agent communication bus, and knowledge is lazy-loaded only when needed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Layer 4 (verification and safeguards): Validator’s independent calculation re-verification, Auditor’s read-only citation audit of the final report, Tracker’s progress tracking, SummarizingConversationManager’s overflow prevention, and selective prompt caching manage both quality and cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each layer forms a defense system that compensates for what the preceding layers miss. When architecture isolation is insufficient, prompts limit output. When prompts are insufficient, tools keep content external. And when problems still arise, verification catches them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each of these layers is documented in Anthropic’s engineering posts, &lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;“Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents”&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/code-execution-with-mcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;“Code Execution with MCP”&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;“How We Built Our Multi-Agent Research System”&lt;/a&gt;. Much of what they recommend already ships in the Strands Agents SDK rather than being something you build: SummarizingConversationManager, prompt caching, and the agents-as-tools pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When building production agents, the LLM’s context window alone is rarely enough. Apply these context engineering techniques to manage context efficiently and scale your agents for production workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deep Insight, the reference implementation used here, is open source. Review the code and extend it for your domain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-deep-insight" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.us-east-1.prod.workshops.aws/workshops/ee17ba6e-edc4-4921-aaf6-ca472841c49b/en-US" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Workshop&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Naomi Moneypenny]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 19:08:44 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI + machine learning]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=54939</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Microsoft and fal partner to expand video generation in Microsoft Foundry, helping developers bring creative ideas into enterprise applications.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-foundry-blog/building-the-next-generation-of-multimodal-ai-experiences-with-fal-in-microsoft-/4570032">Building the next generation of multimodal AI experiences with fal in Microsoft Foundry</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft and fal partner to expand video generation in Microsoft Foundry, helping developers bring creative ideas into enterprise applications.<br>Video workloads do not all start with the same input or aim for the same result. A creative team might explore several interpretations of a written brief. A retailer might turn an approved product image into a short motion concept. An application developer might combine image generation and video generation into a workflow that takes an idea from description to frame to scene. Each scenario brings different requirements for visual quality, motion, creative control, turnaround time, and cost. All of this means that a multi-model solution is necessary to realize a full vision.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Today, Microsoft and fal are helping developers find the right tools for their workloads. We are excited to announce that Kling, LTX, and MiniMax video models are rolling out to in public preview in Microsoft Foundry through our new partnership with fal. Available as a first-party Azure service, these text-to-video and image-to-video options give creators and developers more ways to utilize video generation with the experiences they want to build.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The partnership expands the choices available in Microsoft Foundry while bringing video into the broader platform developers use to build AI applications. The goal is to let developers pick the right model for each step of an experience, then connect those steps into a working application.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">“fal has seen incredible adoption this year alone with generative media across all industries from advertising to marketing to gaming. Partnering with Microsoft allows both of us to truly enable the wide creative ecosystem and ensure that all AI solutions that lean on generative media, becomes a multi-model one.” &#8211; Burkay Gur, Cofounder</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Bringing fal’s generative media focus to enterprise builders<br>fal is the full stack for generative media, built for creators and developers, with APIs for over 1500 models across image, video, audio, 3D, and generative worlds. As a truly encapsulating platform, fal provides inference, training, and infrastructure to run generative media workloads in production, while also providing innovative post trained models that pushes the boundaries on speed and quality.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That specialization matters because generative media is both a creative and an engineering workload. A compelling demo is only the start. Production applications also need to handle real traffic, scale with demand, and balance output quality, latency, and cost.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Through this partnership, developers will be able to access a selected set of video models in Microsoft Foundry, served by fal and sourced from the companies that build them. The initial portfolio gives teams a choice of models for different creative requirements instead of relying on one model for every task, with more models planned over time.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A portfolio for different video experiences<br>The initial portfolio spans Kling v3 Pro, LTX-2.5 Fast, LTX-2.5 Pro, MiniMax H3 Max, and MiniMax H3 Max Turbo. These models give developers different starting points for generating video from text and animating existing images. A team rapidly exploring creative concepts may prioritize speed and cost, while a team producing fewer, higher-fidelity assets may weigh output quality more heavily. Whether an application starts from text or an existing image, and what it needs in output quality and cost, also shapes the choice.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Kling: From a still image to a directed scene</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Kling v3 Pro combines cinematic visuals and fluid motion with native audio generation and custom element support. Its image-to-video capabilities give developers control over how a starting image becomes a moving scene, a fit for applications that prioritize visual direction and motion.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">LTX: One model for fast drafts, one for final output</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For rapid iteration and previews, LTX-2.5 Fast is the speed-optimized option; for high-fidelity final output, LTX-2.5 Pro is the quality-optimized option. Both support text-to-video and image-to-video generation with synchronized audio.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">MiniMax: Another approach to video generation</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">MiniMax H3 Max and MiniMax H3 Max Turbo are post-trained variants of MiniMax H3 focused on prompt adherence, aesthetics, and inference performance. For developers, they provide additional options to evaluate against an application’s creative brief, quality requirements, and performance needs.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">“When building with video models, the details matter: how closely the output follows your prompt, how well it preserves your reference images/videos/audios and how quickly you can try another idea. Kling gives developers control over detailed reference to video generation. LTX offers fast iteration and high-quality output through its Fast and Pro variants. H3 Max brings fal&#8217;s post-training and inference optimizations to improve quality and speed. Having these models available through Microsoft Foundry gives developers more flexibility to choose what works best for the application they are building.&#8221; &#8211; Gökay Aydoğan, Creative Engineer</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Built for enterprise video workloads<br>Bringing video generation into an enterprise application requires more than choosing the right model. Teams need a platform where they can evaluate different options, build with the models that fit their requirements, and operate those experiences alongside the rest of their applications.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The initial portfolio of video models is hosted in the US, and Microsoft Foundry is the first provider to offer a US-hosted Kling endpoint. Key considerations for enterprise teams:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Data residency: For organizations with data residency requirements, hosting location is one factor to weigh. Review each model&#8217;s documented data processing and storage terms to confirm it meets your requirements.<br>Model evaluation: Teams can assess quality, performance, and cost, then connect video generation with the other models and capabilities their applications need.<br>Production readiness: As they move toward production, they can use the evaluation, optimization, governance, security, observability, and infrastructure capabilities supported for their selected offering.<br>Pricing<br>Video generation pricing varies by model and output resolution, giving teams options to balance creative requirements with their application’s budget. The table below shows pricing in USD per minute of generated video for LTX and MiniMax models, making it easier to compare costs across the available options.</p>

<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Get started<br>Ready to start building? Join us at Ignite for BRK256: Video AI at Scale with fal on Microsoft Foundry. Register today!</p>

		<item>
		<title>Stop treating agents as one-off projects and start running them as a continuous discipline</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/stop-treating-agents-as-one-off-projects-and-start-running-them-as-a-continuous-discipline/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/stop-treating-agents-as-one-off-projects-and-start-running-them-as-a-continuous-discipline/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Cyril Belikoff]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 17:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI + machine learning]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=54831</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>AI must be selected, architected, and managed as a continuous business discipline, not a series of isolated projects.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/stop-treating-agents-as-one-off-projects-and-start-running-them-as-a-continuous-discipline/">Stop treating agents as one-off projects and start running them as a continuous discipline</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Transformation begins at the line between an experiment that works and a capability the business depends on. Scaling intelligence requires production capabilities built for sustained business performance, with investments prioritized against financial thresholds, tied to measurable outcomes, instrumented for unit costs, and owned by accountable leaders. This approach manages cost, performance, governance, and reliability as adoption grows.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A new IDC InfoBrief, sponsored by Microsoft, <a href="https://marketingassets.microsoft.com/adobe/assets/urn:aaid:aem:5d3ad527-11a7-4c8a-a95c-0b37adcbbb4b/original/as/Analyst-Report-Scaling-AI-Without-Scaling-Costs.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Scaling AI Without Scaling Costs</a>*, on how organizations are funding and governing AI, shows both the urgency of that shift and the friction holding it back. </p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://marketingassets.microsoft.com/adobe/assets/urn:aaid:aem:5d3ad527-11a7-4c8a-a95c-0b37adcbbb4b/original/as/Analyst-Report-Scaling-AI-Without-Scaling-Costs.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Read the IDC InfoBrief</a></div>
</div>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Nearly 90% of organizations apply financial thresholds before<br>approving AI initiatives.</p>
<cite><a href="https://marketingassets.microsoft.com/adobe/assets/urn:aaid:aem:5d3ad527-11a7-4c8a-a95c-0b37adcbbb4b/original/as/Analyst-Report-Scaling-AI-Without-Scaling-Costs.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Scaling AI Without Scaling Costs</a></cite></blockquote>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Only 17% of organizations build models to project how costs scale in production—even as about 78% prioritize cost efficiency when selecting a model. 86% expect financial results within six to 24 months—roughly one budget cycle to show something real. Together, those findings point to a new operating reality: AI must be selected, architected, and managed as a continuous business discipline, not a series of isolated projects. The market has moved from proving it works to proving it can scale economically.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="four-questions-to-answer-before-your-next-funding-decisionrun-each-candidate-through-four-questions-that-follow-a-scaled-workload-s-life-fund-it-run-it-improve-it-prove-it">Four questions to answer before your next funding decision</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph" id="four-questions-to-answer-before-your-next-funding-decisionrun-each-candidate-through-four-questions-that-follow-a-scaled-workload-s-life-fund-it-run-it-improve-it-prove-it">Run each candidate through four questions that follow a scaled workload&#8217;s life: fund it, run it, improve it, prove it.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading" id="1-how-can-we-predict-and-track-costs-at-scale">1. How can we predict and track costs at scale?</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A pilot proves the idea, rarely the economics: only 17% of organizations model how costs scale in production. Before you fund, project usage at 3 times and 10 times, and make sure spend traces to an application, team, and agent. <a href="https://devblogs.microsoft.com/foundry/build-2026-foundry-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Compare models on cost, quality, and latency</a>, and commit steady demand to reserved capacity. If cost rises with usage while value flattens, you&#8217;ve found an architecture problem while it&#8217;s still cheap to fix.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading" id="2-how-can-we-optimize-model-costs-for-every-request">2. How can we optimize model costs for every request?</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">One successful outcome can take a dozen model requests, so what matters is cost per outcome, not price per token. Ask:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item">Is routine work like classification paying frontier-model prices?</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Are the same instructions reprocessed on every turn?</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Is work that can wait running at a lower-cost tier?</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The goal is the right model for every request, with evaluation proving quality held.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading" id="3-how-can-we-continuously-improve-agent-performance-and-costs">3. How can we continuously improve agent performance and costs?</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Retrieved documents, tool definitions, instructions, and history are often an agent&#8217;s largest cost and a common cause of weak answers. Check:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item">Does the agent retrieve the relevant passage or the whole document?</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Does it carry every tool description on every turn?</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Is the same procedure copied into every agent?</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Does it replay full transcripts instead of remembering what matters?</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-economics-of-agent-optimization-context-engineering-for-enterprise-ai-agents/">Trimming what the model doesn&#8217;t need lowers cost without hurting quality.</a></p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading" id="4-how-can-we-control-spending-and-measure-roi">4. How can we control spending and measure ROI?</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A budget alert reports the fire after it starts; a runaway agent needs a circuit breaker on every request. Cost must tie to the outcome the business pays for, such as a resolved case, so leaders can optimize, scale, or retire an agent. The business owner also needs an evaluation suite, cost and latency budgets, a rollback path, and regular reviews. With 86% of organizations expecting financial results within six to 24 months, that evidence has about one budget cycle to appear.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph" id="all-four-answers-depend-on-evidence-captured-where-ai-runs-per-request-agent-and-outcome-if-each-team-builds-it-by-hand-the-discipline-won-t-survive-the-tenth-agent-it-has-to-come-from-the-platform-why-microsoft-and-how-microsoft-foundry-helps"><strong>All four answers depend on evidence captured where AI runs:</strong> per request, agent, and outcome. If each team builds it by hand, the discipline won&#8217;t survive the tenth agent. It has to come from the platform.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="all-four-answers-depend-on-evidence-captured-where-ai-runs-per-request-agent-and-outcome-if-each-team-builds-it-by-hand-the-discipline-won-t-survive-the-tenth-agent-it-has-to-come-from-the-platform-why-microsoft-and-how-microsoft-foundry-helps">How Microsoft Foundry supports continuous cost optimization</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">When customers ask for a few practical ways to scale AI while controlling costs, I recommend: </p>



<ol class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item">Use model routing across the over 11,000 models available in <a href="https://ai.azure.com/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Foundry</a>.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Implement <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/microsoft-iq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft IQ</a> to improve relevance and reduce costs.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Use the plan, control, and optimize capabilities described below to strengthen operations across the organization.</li>
</ol>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://ai.azure.com/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Foundry</a> is the enterprise platform to build, ground, and govern AI apps and agents at scale. It brings together your full agent lifecycle with open development, built-in intelligence, and consistent security, compliance, and policy controls across every agent. Think of it as a repeatable production line rather than a set of one-off builds, where the same controls, evidence, and economics apply to the tenth agent as to the first.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is what makes the operating loop practical at scale. Across the loop:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Plan:</strong> Compare models on cost, quality, and performance before committing to an architecture, and match predictable baseline demand to reserved capacity while keeping the flexibility to burst. The decisions that set your production cost curve get made with data rather than intuition.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Control</strong>: <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/agent-factory-top-5-agent-observability-best-practices-for-reliable-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Observability</a> that reports cost alongside quality, latency, and safety in one view, so the economics of a workload are visible while it&#8217;s running rather than reconstructed at quarter-end. That&#8217;s what makes cost per outcome a number you can put in front of your CFO.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Optimize:</strong> Model routing that sends each request to the smallest model that can serve it, an intelligence layer that provides context and retrieval centrally and addresses a common source of avoidable token spend, and evaluation tooling that catches quality regression when you make a cost decision—because a cheaper system that quietly degrades isn&#8217;t a saving.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The value compounds with volume. When every agent inherits the same grounding, governance, and cost telemetry from the platform, scaling from one use case to 20 doesn&#8217;t mean rebuilding the discipline 20 times. What <a href="https://ai.azure.com/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Foundry</a> does is make sure the evidence exists when the funding conversation comes around, and the levers are within reach when you need them.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="next-steps-for-optimizing-ai-agent-costs-and-roi">Next steps for optimizing AI agent costs and ROI</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Scaling AI isn&#8217;t a bigger experiment. It&#8217;s a different discipline: a shared threshold, a unit cost, a value curve that outruns the cost curve, and an owner whose name is on the result.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Go deeper with <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/tag/the-economics-of-agent-optimization/">our four-part blog series on The Economics of Agent Optimization</a> in Microsoft Foundry, which takes each of these questions from principle to implementation:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-economics-of-agent-optimization-from-pilots-to-measurable-returns/">AI cost management: From AI pilots to measurable ROI</a></li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-economics-of-agent-optimization-four-ways-to-lower-the-cost/">AI cost optimization: How to lower AI spend</a></li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-economics-of-agent-optimization-context-engineering-for-enterprise-ai-agents/">AI agent optimization: How context engineering lowers AI costs</a></li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-economics-of-agent-optimization-how-ai-agent-governance-controls-cost-and-proves-roi/">AI agent governance: How to measure AI value and ROI</a></li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Then join us at <a href="https://ignite.microsoft.com/en-US/home">Microsoft Ignite</a>, November 17–20 in San Francisco or online, to see it in action and go hands-on with the team that built it.</p>

		<item>
		<title>Designing Azure silicon on Azure silicon: EDA workloads on Cobalt 200</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/designing-azure-silicon-on-azure-silicon-eda-workloads-on-cobalt-200/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/designing-azure-silicon-on-azure-silicon-eda-workloads-on-cobalt-200/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Selim Bilgin and Silvian Goldenberg]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 16:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Compute]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=54813</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Discover how Microsoft is accelerating electronic design automation (EDA) workloads with Azure Cobalt 200 VMs. Explore early performance results demonstrating faster simulation, verification, and static timing analysis, along with the benefits of scalable cloud infrastructure for silicon design and development.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/designing-azure-silicon-on-azure-silicon-eda-workloads-on-cobalt-200/">Designing Azure silicon on Azure silicon: EDA workloads on Cobalt 200</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Every Azure chip Microsoft has built started as electronic design automation (EDA) jobs used to design, test, and validate silicon before it is manufactured. This includes simulation sweeps that run for days, verification regressions that fan out across thousands of cores overnight, and physical signoff runs that use hundreds of gigabytes of memory for a single block. Together, these workloads account for hundreds of millions of compute jobs each year. Today we’re sharing early results from our next step in that evolution: running EDA workloads on <a href="https://aka.ms/cobalt200vms" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><strong>Azure Cobalt 200</strong></a>, Azure’s custom compute infrastructure built on the Arm Neoverse CSS V3. In our silicon design environment, the evaluated EDA workloads ran with <strong>1.30x to 4.15x more performance</strong> than on Cobalt 100 across a range of testing scenarios, from simulation to verification to static timing analysis.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://aka.ms/cobalt200vms" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer noreferrer noopener">Learn more about Azure Cobalt 200 VMs</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Traditionally, EDA workloads ran on fixed, on-premise x86 server capacity, sized for peak demand with periods of underutilization. The shift to cloud-based EDA on Microsoft Azure offered a new level of flexibility, scale, and reliability on compute-intensive silicon design workloads. Using <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/sizes/cobalt-overview" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Azure Cobalt-based virtual machine</a> (VM) instances, silicon engineering teams at Microsoft are experiencing significant performance and efficiency improvements on EDA workloads that Azure customers are seeing across many workloads—optimizing the performance and speed of designing increasingly sophisticated silicon for increasingly complex cloud systems.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">In this blog, we highlight results from Cadence Xcelium and Cadence Spectre, Synopsys PrimeTime and Synopsys VCS, and Siemens Calibre tools. Tools enabled on Cobalt 100 ran unmodified in our Cobalt 200 evaluation, allowing us to focus on performance testing rather than functionality.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="why-cobalt-200-suits-eda">Why Cobalt 200 suits EDA</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">EDA spans workloads with very different compute requirements:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Circuit and analog simulation</strong> — optimized for highly parallel workloads, with sensitivity to memory behavior.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Functional and formal verification</strong> — throughput-driven, with large numbers of independent jobs running concurrently.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Implementation and signoff</strong> — memory-intensive and bandwidth-sensitive, with long-running jobs.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For computer-aided design (CAD) managers, predictable per-core performance is essential to planning overnight regressions. Per-core performance measured on a lightly loaded VM does not necessarily hold as concurrency increases. Verification farms need predictable throughput when VMs are heavily loaded.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Cobalt 200 combines physical cores without the overhead of hyperthreading, dedicated 3MB L2 cache per core, and high provisioned memory bandwidth to support concurrent workloads. These core and memory architecture characteristics support strong per-core performance at high utilization. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="what-we-measured">What we measured</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">We tested Cobalt 200 with workloads from several stages of Microsoft’s silicon development flow, including RTL and gate-level simulation, static timing, physical verification, and analog simulation, comparing job completion time with Cobalt 100 and selected x86 Azure VMs.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">All tested VMs were configured with the maximum number of cores possible for high throughput<sup> 5</sup>, multiple jobs were concurrently executed on the VM to validate the performance under high load conditions. Throughput is normalized per logical core. For the Cobalt 100, Cobalt 200, and hx176rs configurations, logical cores are also physical cores. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="consolidated-eda-workload-results">Consolidated EDA workload results</h2>



<figure class="wp-block-table is-style-stripes"><table class="has-fixed-layout"><thead><tr><th>Workload</th><th>EDA tool</th><th>Measure</th><th>Cobalt 200 result</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><strong>Digital simulation</strong></td><td>Cadence Xcelium<sup>1</sup></td><td>Peak throughput per logical core</td><td><strong>More than 40% higher</strong> than any other tested system</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Digital simulation</strong></td><td>Synopsys VCS<sup>1</sup></td><td>Peak throughput per logical core</td><td><strong>More than 40% higher</strong> than any other tested system</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Analog and mixed-signal simulation</strong></td><td>Cadence Spectre<sup>2</sup></td><td>Throughput per logical core</td><td><strong>38% higher</strong> generation over generation; exceeded tested x86 Azure VMs</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Physical verification</strong></td><td>Siemens Calibre<sup>3</sup> DRC</td><td>Wall time / relative performance</td><td><strong>23% lower</strong> wall time; 1.30× relative performance versus Cobalt 100</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Static timing analysis</strong></td><td>Synopsys PrimeTime<sup>4</sup></td><td>Peak throughput per logical core</td><td><strong>2.0×</strong> Cobalt 100; exceeded tested x86 Azure VMs</td></tr></tbody></table></figure>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="digital-simulation">Digital simulation</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Cadence Xcelium and Synopsys VCS are tools used to simulate and verify digital designs. RTL simulation is central to functional verification and can consume significant compute capacity as teams run large regression suites. When testing RTL workloads using both simulators, optimally loaded Cobalt 200 systems delivered significantly greater peak throughput than the previous generation. For the workload tested on Xcelium and VCS, Cobalt 200 delivered more than <strong>40% higher throughput per logical core than any other system we tested</strong>.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Throughput is normalized per logical core to account for the VMs’ different core counts.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Cobalt 200 was optimized for EDA workloads through increased cache size and improved memory bandwidth per core. Our results demonstrate that Cobalt 200 design optimizations generate significantly improved performance for logic simulation workloads. </p>



<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6aef936320ca9&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6aef936320ca9" class="wp-block-image aligncenter size-large wp-lightbox-container"><img fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" width="1024" height="575" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence-Xcelium-RTLSimulation-Throughput-1-1024x575.jpg" alt="Bar chart comparing Cadence Xcelium RTL simulation throughput, normalized to Azure Cobalt 100. <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="analog-and-mixed-signal-simulation">Analog and mixed signal simulation</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Cadence Spectre is used for analog and mixed-signal circuit simulation. Analog and mixed-signal circuit simulation workloads differ from RTL simulation and are dominated by multithreaded performance. We tested Cadence Spectre on Cobalt 200 and observed <strong>38% higher throughput per logical core </strong>generation over generation, and delivered 11-52% higher throughput per logical core compared with all other platforms tested. </p>



<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6aef936321f0f&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6aef936321f0f" class="wp-block-image aligncenter size-large wp-lightbox-container"><img decoding="async" width="1024" height="570" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Cadence-Spectre-Analog-Simulation-Throughput-1-1024x570.jpg" alt="Bar chart comparing Cadence Spectre analog simulation throughput, normalized to Azure Cobalt 100. <p class="wp-block-paragraph">This result shows meaningful progress between Cobalt generations. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="physical-verification">Physical verification</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Siemens Calibre DRC checks a design’s physical layout against manufacturing design rules before tapeout.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For the Calibre workload we tested DRC against an IP Macro with 16 cores per job, Cobalt 200 completed the run in approximately 23% lower wall time than Cobalt 100, equivalent to 1.30x relative performance. It also outperformed the selected x86 Azure VMs at the same per-job core count.</p>


<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6aef936322e85&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6aef936322e85" class="wp-block-image aligncenter size-large wp-lightbox-container"><img decoding="async" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/261102_Cobalt-EDA_Blog-1024x576.webp" alt="Bar chart comparing Siemens Calibre DRC performance using 16 cores, normalized to Azure Cobalt 100. <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="static-timing-analysis-on-synopsys-primetime">Static timing analysis on Synopsys PrimeTime</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Synopsys PrimeTime is used for static timing analysis to verify that a design meets its timing requirements. For the PrimeTime workload tested with 16 cores per job, Cobalt 200 delivered 2x the peak throughput per logical core of Cobalt 100 and exceeded the selected x86 Azure VMs. Figure 5 compares each VM platform’s highest measured throughput value, normalized to Cobalt 100.</p>



<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6aef9363237f0&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6aef9363237f0" class="wp-block-image aligncenter size-large wp-lightbox-container"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" width="1024" height="570" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys-PrimeTime-Static-Timing-Throughput-1024x570.jpg" alt="Bar chart comparing Synopsys PrimeTime static timing throughput, normalized to Azure Cobalt 100. 			</svg>
		</button><figcaption class="wp-element-caption"><em>Figure 5. Synopsys PrimeTime peak job throughput for an internal test case, using 16 cores per job and normalized to jobs per hour per logical core; Cobalt 100 = 1.0.</em> </figcaption></figure>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="more-than-just-an-soc-platform-level-advantages-to-cobalt-200">More than just an SoC: Platform-level advantages to Cobalt 200</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A higher-performance compute SoC only helps if the rest of the platform scales its performance as well. EDA workloads depend on high-bandwidth and low-latency storage and data movement, and demand varies throughout a silicon program. Verification activity can spike before a milestone, while physical verification and signoff activity increase toward tapeout. Demand can fall sharply once those milestones are completed.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Running on Azure pairs Cobalt 200 compute with:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Elastic capacity</strong>: provisioning compute capacity for a regression campaign or signoff push, then release it as demand falls. Size for the workload, not the annual peak.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>High-performance storage: </strong>supporting the data demands of large design databases, waveform dumps, and physical verification runs.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Global capacity</strong>: aligning deployments with engineering locations, data residency requirements, and regional availability.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Adjacency to Azure AI services</strong>: As agentic and AI-assisted design workflows evolve, proximity between AI inference services and Cobalt 200 compute creates opportunities for tighter integration of AI-powered reasoning with EDA execution. This <strong>reduces</strong> data movement overhead, accelerates iterative design and verification cycles, and enables AI agents to orchestrate complex engineering workflows more efficiently.</li>
</ul>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft is a key partner for Cadence, and our collaboration on Cobalt spans multiple generations of Azure silicon. With Cadence EDA workflows now qualified on Cobalt 200, we are extending that collaboration to the latest generation of Azure infrastructure and giving semiconductor teams greater flexibility in how they access and scale compute for demanding design and verification workloads.</p>
<cite><strong>—Paul Cunningham, Sr. VP &amp; GM, System Verification Group, Cadence</strong></cite></blockquote>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As semiconductor designs grow increasingly complex, engineering teams face mounting pressure to accelerate silicon development while managing growing compute demands. Executing Siemens EDA solutions on Microsoft Azure Cobalt 200 provides an efficient, scalable platform for demanding verification and signoff workloads. Our collaboration with Microsoft demonstrates the value of matching the right workloads to the right compute architecture to advance silicon innovation.</p>
<cite><strong>—Craig Johnson, VP of Cloud Solutions, Siemens EDA</strong></cite></blockquote>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Our longstanding partnership with Microsoft is grounded in a shared commitment to help engineering teams tackle increasingly complex design challenges. We congratulate Microsoft on the impressive performance of Cobalt 200, developed using Synopsys’ comprehensive EDA flow spanning verification, implementation and signoff, together with HAPS hardware prototyping and Synopsys IP. We’re excited to see Cobalt 200 delivering meaningful performance gains generation-on-generation, including more than 40% faster runtimes with Synopsys VCS and 2x per-core throughput with Synopsys PrimeTime. We look forward to continuing our collaboration to help engineering teams advance the next generation of AI silicon.</p>
<cite><strong>—Sanjay Bali, Senior Vice President of EDA Product Management and Strategy at Synopsys</strong></cite></blockquote>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="designing-next-generation-cobalt-with-cobalt-200">Designing Next-Generation Cobalt with Cobalt 200</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">There’s a virtuous cycle between silicon development and EDA performance: EDA tools helped bring Cobalt 200 to life, and now Cobalt 200 is accelerating those same design and verification workloads for our next-generation Cobalt products. By observing how EDA workloads perform at scale, we gain insights that help shape future generations of Cobalt and Azure compute infrastructure, with each generation helping design and improve the next. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="what-comes-next">What comes next</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Our early tests show significant progress for EDA on Cobalt 200. Existing Arm-enabled tools ran without workflow changes, and the tested workloads performed competitively against the selected x86 Azure VMs. Xcelium and PrimeTime showed a particularly large improvement over Cobalt 100. Spectre and Calibre which were strong performers on Cobalt 100 also improved generation over generation, and we are working with our EDA partners on further tuning of multiple tools.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">We are broadening workload coverage, validating results on a greater scale, and continuing to collaborate with our EDA partners as Cobalt 200 enters general availability.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">If you&#8217;re evaluating cloud infrastructure for semiconductor design, we invite you to test your EDA workflows on <a href="https://aka.ms/cobalt200vms" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Cobalt 200 VMs</a>. </p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><em>Learn more about Cobalt at Microsoft Ignite: </em><a href="https://ignite.microsoft.com/en-US/sessions/1787856640202001EbGn-1791588234328001CsHm?source=sessions" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><em>Cobalt 200: Delivering Azure&#8217;s Best Performance And Efficiency</em></a></li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><em>Check out <a href="https://aka.ms/silicontosystems" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft’s silicon to system approach to infrastructure</a>.</em></li>
</ul>
